    LATTICEBLINDFOLD: A LATTICE-BASED ANALOGUE OF NOVABLINDFOLD



                                                                              LUCA DALL’AVA

                                                                                  ICME Labs
                                                                             https://www.icme.io

            Abstract. Folding schemes compress many instances of a relation into a single accumulated one and,
            via composition with the Fiat–Shamir heuristic, yield SNARKs for arbitrarily large computations. How-
            ever, essentially every folding scheme beyond Nova itself (including the lattice-based SuperNeo [NS26],
            LatticeFold(+) [BC24, BC25], and Cyclo [GLLO26]) is only randomizing, not blinding (i.e. honest-verifier
            zero-knowledge): its folding transcript leaks information about the witnesses being folded. We present
            LatticeBlindFold, a first lattice-based, plausibly post-quantum-secure analogue of the NovaBlindFold pro-
            tocol [KS23, KS25], obtained by making SuperNeo blinding. This is a first step, intended to establish
            feasibility; for simplicity we restrict to the interactive setting. We also hope the note serves as a record of
            the difficulties one encounters in achieving zero-knowledge for lattice-based folding schemes.
                Our central technical device is the ABDLOP commitment scheme [BDL+ 16, LNP22], used as a commit-
            and-prove backbone. We mask SuperNeo’s Sum-Check transcript via Libra-style polynomial masking, re-
            place its plaintext evaluation hints with ABDLOP commitments checked homomorphically, and employ
            rejection sampling so that the randomized folded instance-witness pair, salts included, is simulatable. Since
            ABDLOP is only known to be secure over the base ring RF and not over the extension ring RK that Super-
            Neo’s Sum-Check runs over, we give a component-wise instantiation of ABDLOP over RK , translating every
            relation into a pair of RF -relations once, at the level of public parameters. The resulting protocol is com-
            plete, knowledge-sound, and blinding (when a single fresh R1CS instance is folded), with security reducing
            to Module-SIS/Module-LWE assumptions over cyclotomic rings (together with the Extended-MLWE variant
            of [LNS20]). Completeness moves from perfect completeness to a statistical one, due to the introduction of
            rejection sampling. The price of blinding is paid through the parameters rather than the asymptotics. Re-
            jection sampling forces a norm-decomposition depth k = Θ(log nF ), whereas SuperNeo needs only k = Θ(1).
            We record as a corollary an accumulator-free variant, dropping the prover-sampled blinding pairs that the
            main protocol carries for interface compatibility with ΠSuperNeo , which saves a few decomposition digits
            (k = 26 rather than 31 at our parameters) at no cost in blinding. At a matched k the two schemes agree
            in prover time, verifier time, and communication up to constant factors, but measured against SuperNeo at
            its native parameters LatticeBlindFold carries a Θ(log nF ) multiplicative overhead in all three. Once again,
            all security figures we quote are interactive; a Fiat–Shamir transform instantiation would require a higher
            degree fields while here we focus on degree 2 for simplicity of exposition. LatticeBlindFold inherits Super-
            Neo’s compatibility with small-field arithmetic and offers a modular, plausibly post-quantum-safe route to
            introducing zero-knowledge on top of a SNARK. We stress the shape of what we prove here: ΠLBF is a single
            interactive step, taking its input uncommitted and outputting k committed evaluation claims together with
            the ABDLOP openings certifying them. Turning it into a deployable wrapper requires a decider for those
            claims, whether by having a downstream verifier consume them directly or by arithmetizing the ABDLOP
            verification; we do not construct one here, and neither recursive composition nor the instance-in/instance-out
            folding interface is claimed. This work is directly motivated by the Jolt Atlas zkML framework [BCDG26].

                                                                                1. Introduction
1.1. Motivation. The intersection of zero-knowledge (ZK) proofs and succinct argument systems is a cen-
tral and rapidly evolving focus of modern cryptography: enabling one party to convince another that a
                                              LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                  3


computation was performed correctly, without revealing the underlying data, while keeping the proof itself
cheap to produce and to verify. A particularly fruitful framework for this line of work is that of folding
schemes, as introduced by Nova [KST22, KS23]. Folding schemes compress many instances of a relation into
a single accumulated instance which can then be checked; if this accumulated instance is verified, then also
the instances we started with are (up to negligible probability) verified. Interestingly, repeated application
of a folding step yields (informally and with composition with the Fiat–Shamir heuristic) a SNARK: the
prover reduces an arbitrarily large computation to a single, efficiently checkable instance. Nova and its de-
scendants (HyperNova [KS23], SuperNeo [NS26]) all follow this paradigm. Furthermore, the Nova scheme,
in its NovaBlindFold protocol instantiation (as formalized in [KS23, KS25]) provides a systematic way of
introducing zero-knowledge into this process, without stacking a separate ZK argument system on top of an
already-existing SNARK. This is the scheme that we generalize here to the lattice-based framework.
   This second application of folding schemes is an active line of research; recent efforts to add zero-knowledge
on top of existing SNARKs include NovaBlindFold [KS23, KS25] and VEIL [DHRR26].
   Our own interest in this question is concrete rather than purely theoretical. At ICME Labs we de-
velop Jolt Atlas [BCDG26], a zero-knowledge machine learning (zkML) framework built on top of the Jolt
zkVM [AST23], in which zero-knowledge is currently obtained via the NovaBlindFold trick recalled below.
Let us remark that by zero-knowledge we always mean honest-verifier zero-knowledge (HVZK), and we adopt
this convention throughout. The increasingly pressing need for a post-quantum-safe transition, together with
the computational benefits of working over small fields, led us to ask whether the same blinding trick could
be transplanted from the elliptic-curve setting of Nova to a lattice-based, SuperNeo-style architecture. An-
swering this question is the starting point of the present paper, and we return to this motivating application
in §1.2.1 once the relevant background is in place.
   With the notable exception of Nova itself, however, existing folding schemes do not achieve zero-knowledge
out of the box. The transcript produced during a folding step leaks information about the witnesses being
folded. NovaBlindFold addresses the following natural question:
        Can we obtain a blinding (i.e. zero-knowledge) folding scheme without introducing a separate
        ZK argument layer on top?
The answer is yes, and the mechanism is remarkably simple. Recall the following informal definition, which
we make precise in Definition 2.11 below.

Definition (Blinding (Informal)). A folding reduction Π : R × R′ → R is blinding if there exist a sampling
algorithm sample and a simulator S such that the interaction transcript is computationally indistinguishable
from a simulated one, given only the instances (never the witnesses).

  Concretely, blinding for the Nova protocol is achieved as follows:
    (1) The prover samples a randomized instance-witness pair (ur , wr ) ← sample, independently of the real
        witness (u, w) it wishes to hide.
    (2) It folds the two pairs ((ur , wr ), (u, w)) together; by linearity of the folding operation, the output
        witness is wout = ρ · wr + w for a verifier challenge ρ.
    (3) Because wr is freshly random and ρ is a verifier challenge unknown to the prover at sampling time,
        wout is computationally indistinguishable from a fresh sample of sample.
The prover can therefore transmit wout in the clear. No hiding commitment is required, since the randomiza-
tion is already baked into the folding step itself. This yields zero-knowledge on top of essentially any SNARK,
provided the underlying folding scheme satisfies the blinding property, i.e. that the output instance-witness
distribution is independent of the real witness whenever one of the two inputs being folded is freshly random.

Remark. The Nova folding scheme is blinding. HyperNova, however, satisfies only a strictly weaker prop-
erty, called randomizing: the folded instance-witness pair is indistinguishable from a randomly sampled pair,
but this alone does not imply zero-knowledge.

   This difference leaves a crucial gap. Almost all widely used lattice-based folding schemes, including
LatticeFold [BC24], LatticeFold+ [BC25], SuperNeo [NS26], and the more recent Cyclo [GLLO26], are
generalizations of HyperNova rather than of Nova, and consequently inherit the same obstruction: they are
sound and complete, but not blinding.
4                                               LATTICEBLINDFOLD


    The one lattice-based exception we are aware of is Lova [FKNP24], a direct adaptation of Nova whose
folding step is linear in exactly the sense the blinding trick requires; it does not, however, achieve zero-
knowledge, and it builds on unstructured SIS over Z/264 Z rather than on the module setting. We take the
SuperNeo route instead: our motivating application (§1.2.1) is Sum-Check-based and operates over small
fields, and SuperNeo’s reduction is Sum-Check-native over RF = F[X]/(Φ(X)), which lets us plug ΠLBF
in without re-encoding the constraint system, whereas Z/264 Z is not an integral domain and carries no
multilinear extension to blind. The ABDLOP commitment scheme [LNP22] and its ZKPoK, on which all of
§4 rests, are module-lattice objects, so transplanting them to the Lova setting would mean re-deriving the
whole commit-and-prove layer. A zero-knowledge Lova would be a genuinely different point in the design
space, and we regard it as an interesting open question.
    Two further recent lattice-based folding schemes are SALSAA [KLOT25], whose folding application oper-
ates natively on ℓ2 -norm-bounded witnesses (and whose exact norm-check is of independent interest here, cf.
Remark 4.8), and PikkuFold [Osa26], which also highlights the necessity of obtaining zero-knowledge folding
schemes. Neither claims a blinding property.
    Throughout the paper, we focus on SuperNeo [NS26] as it is the protocol we build on; however, we believe
that our modifications can be applied to similar Sum-Check-based folding schemes. SuperNeo is a folding
scheme for the norm-bounded CCS relation CCS(b, L) ([NS26, Definition 19], recalled as Definition 2.21
below) over the cyclotomic ring RF = F[X]/(Φ(X)), where F is a small field and Φ a cyclotomic polynomial.
It achieves knowledge soundness and completeness, but its blinding property fails at exactly two points:
    (1) The Sum-Check protocol: SuperNeo’s core reduction relies on the Sum-Check protocol to verify
        that the constraint polynomial vanishes on the Boolean hypercube. The transcript of a (plain)
        Sum-Check execution reveals partial evaluations of this polynomial at each round, which encode
        information about the underlying witness.
    (2) The evaluation hints: At the end of the Sum-Check phase, the prover sends hint values
                               ′     ]         ′
                              yi,j = M̄ j zi (r ) ∈ RK ,   ∀ i ∈ [K + k], ∀ j ∈ [t],
        for a suitable extension field K/F. These are linear functions of the witnesses zi evaluated at a
        random point r′ ∈ Klog2 (m) , and are sent to the verifier in the clear.
Neither leak is fatal in a single execution, but an honest-but-curious verifier who accumulates these quantities
across many folding rounds can recover substantial structural information about the witnesses. That is
precisely the situation that NovaBlindFold was designed to rule out in the elliptic-curve setting, and that we
rule out here in the lattice setting.
   Let us stress that a recurring theme throughout our construction is that every quantity that would
otherwise leak information is routed through the ABDLOP commitment scheme [BDL+ 16, LNP22]. Unlike
the succinct Ajtai commitment SuperNeo already relies on, ABDLOP is non-succinct but computationally
hiding and comes equipped with an efficient zero-knowledge proof of knowledge, which lets us replace every
point where SuperNeo discloses a value in the clear with a commit-and-prove step instead.

1.2. LatticeBlindFold. The goal of this paper is to close this gap. We construct LatticeBlindFold, a
blinding procedure based on a folding scheme over lattices, i.e. the lattice-based analogue of NovaBlindFold.
Let us remark that, to the best of our knowledge no lattice-based folding procedure has been shown to satisfy
a blinding property in the sense of Definition 2.11. Beyond recovering zero-knowledge, working over lattices
rather than elliptic curves brings two further, largely independent, advantages, which we discuss in turn.
     • Small-field arithmetic: SuperNeo operates over fields F of roughly 61–64 bits and quadratic
       extensions K of roughly 122–128 bits, rather than the 254-bit prime-order field required by elliptic-
       curve-based SNARKs such as Nova. Constraint systems that are naturally expressed over small fields
       (as is typical of zkVM lookup arguments) benefit directly from this.
     • Plausible post-quantum security: Every hardness assumption we rely on reduces to Ring-
       SIS/Module-SIS over RF , believed to be hard even against quantum adversaries. This is in sharp
       contrast to NovaBlindFold, whose security rests on the hardness of the discrete logarithm problem,
       a problem that Shor’s algorithm breaks in polynomial time on a quantum computer. This concern
       is not merely theoretical; see e.g. [BZG+ 26] on the concrete quantum risk to elliptic-curve-based
       cryptography.
                                              LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                 5


   These advantages come at a cost, though a subtler one than might be expected. None of the machinery
blinding requires (the masked Sum-Check, the ABDLOP commitments to the evaluation hints, the batched
anchoring proofs of knowledge, the degree-0 surrogates and their constant-coefficient proofs) changes the
order of prover time, verifier time or communication at a fixed decomposition parameter k. The additive
overhead is the public data of the instances the prover introduces, namely, the mask’s public instance and
that of the k sampled blinding pairs, of size nF,in each (the k pairs share a single one, since splitb and Lin
commute), together with the k compact Ajtai commitments cblind   i    and the kt evaluation hints those pairs
carry. The last two are Θ(k), hence of the same order as the k output instances that any decomposition-based
folding step already transmits. They appear in the constant on the log2 (m) term of Table 1, not its order, but
they are real bytes and are charged as such. The price is paid through k itself. Rejection sampling inflates
                               √
the norm bound by a factor nF , forcing k = Θ(log nF ) where ΠSuperNeo may take k = Θ(1). Therefore,
when each protocol is read at its own parameters, ΠLBF costs a Θ(log nF ) multiplicative factor in all three
metrics, and this in turn feeds into the concrete security estimates. We report both schemes at a matched k
in Table 1 so that the two columns describe the same protocol structure and can be read metric by metric,
but the matched-k reading understates the true gap and should not be taken as the operative comparison.
We make this precise in §4.3.3, and in particular in Remark 4.18.

1.2.1. Applications. As mentioned above, our primary motivation for developing LatticeBlindFold is to
provide a modular, plausibly post-quantum-safe way to introduce zero-knowledge on top of the Jolt At-
las architecture [BCDG26], a zero-knowledge machine learning (zkML) framework that extends the Jolt
zkVM [AST23] (and proving system) to model inference, developed by ICME Labs. Jolt Atlas currently
obtains zero-knowledge via the NovaBlindFold trick; the increasingly pressing need for a post-quantum-safe
transition, together with the computational benefits of small-field arithmetic discussed above, motivated
us to seek a lattice-based analogue of this trick that could be plugged into a SuperNeo-based (rather than
Nova-based) architecture.
   We stress, however, that the relevance of this construction extends well beyond Jolt Atlas specifically.
The modularity of the blinding-folding paradigm, namely the fact that zero-knowledge can be bolted onto
an existing (non-ZK) SNARK without redesigning it from scratch, is a central topic for modern cryptographic
applications generally. Any system built on a HyperNova-style folding scheme, lattice-based or not, faces the
same witness-leakage obstruction we describe above, and stands to benefit from the same kind of modular
fix. This direction is not idiosyncratic to our own work: a growing number of teams are exploring lattice-
based zkVM architectures, and LatticeBlindFold is designed to fit naturally within that broader shift toward
plausible post-quantum, modular proof systems, rather than being tailored narrowly to a single application.

1.3. Our contributions. We summarize here the main steps and contributions of this work; the technical
terms below are all defined precisely in the body of the paper, and a reader mainly interested in the high-level
picture can safely skip ahead to §1.4.
     • We introduce a norm-bounded committed CCS evaluation relation CEcom (b, L, Commit) (Defini-
       tion 3.21), its restricted version (Definition 3.25), as well as a Relaxed committed CCS evaluation re-
       lation which augment SuperNeo’s evaluation relation with ABDLOP commitments to the evaluation
       hints and their salts (Definition 3.22), a relaxed committed CCS evaluation relation for intermediate
       constructions (Definition 3.24), and padded/unpadded layouts (Definition 3.28). The reductions can
       then check consistency without ever seeing the hints in the clear; the restricted/unpadded relations
       are what makes zero-knowledge attainable. Due to all these modifications, at the same time, we
       adjust the setting of interactive reductions introduced in [NS26] to our needs.
     • Even though we keep considering the compact Ajtai commitment scheme, we need to introduce an
       extra blinding block in order to achieve (statistical) hiding (Definition 3.1, Lemma 3.3) alongside the
       relaxed binding property (Definition 2.27).
     • We mask the Sum-Check transcript with a Libra-style polynomial p(X)      ⃗ = a0 +Pℓ p̃i (Xi ) [XZZ+ 19].
                                                                                           i=1
       Unlike loc. cit., whose verifier reconstructs the unmasked evaluation Q(⃗r ′ ) once the mask’s own eval-
       uation is opened by the polynomial commitment scheme (Remark 3.18.(1)), we reveal neither: the
       final check takes place entirely at the level of the commitments, so p(⃗r ′ ) is never opened and Q(⃗r ′ )
       never formed as a field element in V’s view. The complete inventory of what leaves the prover in the
       clear is §4.1.1.1.
6                                            LATTICEBLINDFOLD


     • We replace SuperNeo’s plaintext evaluation hints with a commit-and-prove step using the ABDLOP
       commitment scheme [BDL+ 16, LNP22], and show that equality checks on the hints can instead be
       performed homomorphically at the level of the commitments.
     • Since ABDLOP and its zero-knowledge proof of knowledge [LNP22] are only known to be secure
       natively over RF , while SuperNeo’s Sum-Check transcript lives over RK , we cannot invoke them over
       RK off the shelf. We give instead a component-wise instantiation (§3.3): writing K = F[Y ]/(Y 2 − ν)
       for a quadratic non-residue ν, we commit to the {1, Y }-coordinates of RK -elements directly as RF -
       elements and translate every RK -relation into an equivalent pair of RF -relations once, at the level of
       public parameters, before invoking [LNP22]’s proof system unchanged.
     • Several equalities we must certify are not identities in RK but constrain only the constant coefficient of
       a committed ring element: this is the case for the degree-0 surrogates ŷi,j , which force the committed
       hints into the base field before the degree-two R1CS check, and for the anchoring proofs binding
       the Sum-Check output to the committed values. Since [LNP22]’s system certifies full-ring linear
       relations u = R1 s1 + Rm m for a public u, it cannot be invoked on them directly. We therefore
                                                             (ct)
       isolate a vanishing-constant-coefficient wrapper Πmany (§3.2.1), in the style of [LNP22, §3.2, §4.3],
       aggregating an arbitrary family of such statements into a single full-ring relation at additive cost
       1/|K|, a single execution sufficing because our challenges lie in K = Fq2 .
     • As a by-product of that aggregation, batching the degree-0 checks with independent per-index chal-
       lenges avoids the union bound one would otherwise pay, giving soundness error 2/|K| rather than
       (K + k)t/|K| (Lemma 3.13). Separating the three batched families with independent challenges,
       rather than with powers of a single γ, likewise improves the Schwartz–Zippel error of the batching
       (cf. [NS26, Appendix B.2]).
     • We commit to the masking polynomial by applying ABDLOP directly to its coefficients, rather
       than through a polynomial commitment scheme supporting coefficients in K; our reasons for such
       approach are discussed in §3.5.
     • Handling commitments comes with a cost. We give a modified random-linear-combination reduction
       which, rather than decomposing the ABDLOP salts alongside the witnesses, resamples them at
       every decomposition step and certifies the pieces by a batched proof of knowledge; this keeps every
       accumulator commitment MLWE-hiding, decouples the salt budget from the decomposition depth k,
       and lowers the bound at which binding must be assumed. Simulatability of the resulting randomized
       pair, and hence blinding, further requires the rejection sampling of [LNP22, §2.6] recalled in §3.6,
       whose repetition overhead is a small (≈ 2–3) constant factor.
     • A subtlety specific to the interactive setting: an aborted rejection-sampling attempt is not discarded
       but remains in the verifier’s view, and, conditioned on rejection, the mask depends on the very
       witness it is meant to hide. Since the mask’s Ajtai image is not hiding, we commit to it under
       ABDLOP and open it only on acceptance, so that each rejected round carries hiding commitments
       alone while the mask stays bound before the folding challenges are drawn (Remark B.9). The issue
       disappears under Fiat–Shamir, where aborted attempts are never output, or once we compose the
       machinery with the statistical hiding Ajtai commitment.
     • We give a single consolidated expression for the completeness error (Proposition 4.15) and the
       knowledge-soundness error of the composed protocol (Proposition 4.17), rather than leaving the
       various contributions scattered across the individual reductions.
     • Composing these pieces, we obtain LatticeBlindFold (Protocol 12), a folding scheme step that is
       complete, knowledge-sound, and blinding, built entirely from plausible post-quantum (Ring/Module-
       SIS/LWE) assumptions, and represents the analogue of NovaBlindFold.
     • Finally, we isolate as a corollary (Corollary 4.24) an accumulator-free variant Π◦LBF , obtained by
       deleting the k blinding pairs the prover samples. These contribute nothing to hiding, which is
       carried entirely by the Gaussian mask and the blinding block, and are retained in Protocol 12 only
       so that its input and output types match those of ΠSuperNeo and of the folding scheme blueprint of
       §4.2.1.

Theorem A (LatticeBlindFold, informal: Theorem 4.13, Corollary 4.24). Let F be a small prime field of
order q, let K = Fq2 , and let RF = F[X]/(Φ(X)) be a cyclotomic ring of degree d. Let L be the succinct Ajtai
commitment that SuperNeo commits witnesses under, let Commit be the ABDLOP commitment scheme, and
                                              LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                  7


let C ⊂ RF be a strong sampling set with expansion factor T . Assume MSIS and MLWE are hard over RF .
Then the LatticeBlindFold step ΠLBF = Π′DEC ◦ Π′RLC ◦ Π′R1CS (Protocol 12, Definition 4.6) is an interactive
reduction
                    ΠLBF : CCS◦ (b)K ,→ CCS(b, L)K −→ CEcom (b, L, B̃, Commit, T, K)k ,
folding K fresh R1CS instance-witness pairs, together with k accumulator instances the prover samples itself,
into k committed evaluation claims of norm bound b, such that:
   (1) (Completeness.) It is statistically complete, with error εLBF
                                                                 c   ≤ 9 · 2−λ uniformly in k and t.
   (2) (Knowledge soundness.) It is a reduction of knowledge, with error
                          O(log2 m)   Wmax (K + k)             
                εLBF ≤              +              + O (K + k)t · εABDLOP + εbind ,
                             |K|          |C|
       where εABDLOP collects the knowledge-soundness errors of the ABDLOP proofs of knowledge and
       εbind the (relaxed) binding errors of L and Commit. It is negligible in λ whenever MSIS is hard at
       the relevant bounds and |K|, |C| are superpolynomial.
   (3) (Blinding.) If K = 1 the reduction is blinding in the sense of Definition 2.11: the verifier’s entire
       view, aborted rejection-sampling attempts included, is simulatable from the public instances alone,
       with simulator advantage at most εLBF-blind .
Moreover, at a matched decomposition parameter k (cf. Remark 4.18), the prover time, verifier time, and
communication of ΠLBF agree asymptotically with those of ΠSuperNeo up to constant factors, the additive
overhead being the nF,in field elements of the blinding mask’s public instance, the public data of the k blinding
pairs the prover samples (a further nF,in field elements for all k together, plus Θ(k) commitments, which the
Θ(k) already present in both schemes absorbs into the constants), and the blinded layout of Definition 3.1
(at the worst nF doubles).
The three clauses are Theorem 4.13, quantified by Propositions 4.15 and 4.17; the efficiency claim is §4.3.3.
   Ten restrictions on the construction above are worth flagging immediately, since they qualify the paper’s
central claim.
    (1) Even though the protocol can be stated and proved to be a reduction of knowledge for general CCS
        instances, we decide to focus on the R1CS setting (more precisely on those constraint systems arising
        from circuits) for efficiency reasons; cf. Remark 4.1.
    (2) We prove that ΠLBF (Protocol 12) is blinding (Definition 2.11) only when K = 1, i.e. exactly one
        genuine R1CS instance is folded per invocation, alongside k simulated ones, and only for one step
        (not a full folding scheme). Therefore, we deduce that ΠLBF (Protocol 12) introduces zero-knowledge.
        Folding K > 1 genuine instances simultaneously is complete and knowledge-sound but its blinding
        property is not established here; see Theorem 4.13 and §4.1.1.1.
    (3) Security is proved in the interactive setting. Under Fiat–Shamir the 1/|K|-type terms degrade by
        the random-oracle query bound Q ≈ 280 , and a quadratic extension no longer suffices at any Q
        of cryptographic interest. The non-interactive deployment path therefore requires a higher exten-
        sion degree, K = Fq4 at q = 264 − 59; this is quantified in §4.5, and it is a requirement on the
        parameters rather than an open problem (the extension degree is, however, the easy half, while
        the non-interactive figures of that section all assume round-by-round knowledge soundness of the
        Sum-Check-plus-ABDLOP composition, which we do not prove and which the generic multi-round
        bound cannot substitute for at 21 + NPoK challenge rounds; cf. item (2) of §5). We instantiate
        the protocol in two concrete examples, d = 64 and d = 128, achieving ≈ 116 and ≈ 121 bits of
        security respectively (λ = 120, §4.3.4). The two examples must be considered as illustrative, since
        the blinding error εLBF-blind ≤ 2−112 ; cf. Remark 4.23.
    (4) Our efficiency claim is stated at a matched k. Read against ΠSuperNeo at its native k = Θ(1), blinding
        costs a Θ(log nF ) factor in prover time, verifier time and communication (Remark 4.18). We regard
        reducing this to something deployable as the main open engineering problem the paper leaves.
    (5) Our completeness and blinding claims are proved for a single folding step (as this fits our main
        use-case). Each step is only statistically, not perfectly, complete. The error comes from the attempt-
        budget caps on every rejection-sampling loop the protocol runs, namely the mask loop of Π′RLC
        (Remark 4.3) together with the loop internal to each ABDLOP proof-of-knowledge invocation, and
8                                              LATTICEBLINDFOLD


         is 9 · 2−λ under the batched default (Proposition 4.15; the counting convention, and the more con-
         servative unbatched figure, are fixed once in §4.1.2.1). We stress that this is an abort probability for
         the honest prover which vanishes under Fiat–Shamir once the attempt caps are lifted (as no verifier
         is present to enforce them), at the price of an expected- rather than strictly-polynomial-time prover
         (Remark 2.12). Note that this is a statement about completeness only and that the same caps carry
         a knowledge-soundness role that Fiat–Shamir does not remove).
     (6) The relaxation of Definition 3.22 compounds across rounds. Each round of the blueprint Protocol 13
         ΠLBF−Fold contributes a factor c̄ from Lemma 3.6(2) and a factor (ρi − ρ′i ) from the coordinate-wise
         inversion of Lemma 2.19, so the number of sequential rounds the extraction supports is O(1), and
         small (Remark 4.8). Once again, a full treatment of recursive folding, i.e. n sequential rounds as in
         an IVC scheme, is out of scope of this paper, but we wish to mention that this is a limitation for
         obtaining such a folding scheme, which is not merely confined to the proof technique: an IVC use of
         ΠLBF−Fold requires the exact ℓ2 -norm certificate on the fresh salts sketched there, which we do not
         instantiate.
     (7) We consider, throughout the paper, a prime q ≡ 5 (mod 8) and consider a cyclotomic ring of the
         form Z[X]/(xd + 1) for d a power of 2; we believe that most of the discussions can be generalized to
         a general cyclotomic ring, but we do not discuss them here.
     (8) Every R1CS structure and witness below follows the blinded layout of Definition 3.1, so that the
         input instance component c1 = L(z1 ) is hiding (Lemma 3.3). This is what upgrades the blinding
         property of Theorem 4.13 to a zero-knowledge statement, but the layout does not do it alone: the
         upgrade is stated over the unpadded relation of Definition 3.28, the padding being applied by the
         prover as the embedding of Equation (3.19). Stated instead over the committed, already-padded
         relation, the layout buys nothing, since the adversary then supplies the blinding block itself and may
         set it to zero (Remark 3.27).
                                                                                                      (1)
     (9) What ΠLBF produces is k instances of CEcom (b, L, B̃, Commit, T, K) together with the NPoK ≈ 102
         ABDLOP openings that certify them, some 5–8 MB at the parameters of §4.3.4. Someone must still
         check those claims. Two routes exist, and we instantiate neither in this work: either the downstream
         verifier consumes the openings as they are, at that communication cost, or the ABDLOP verification
         equation is arithmetized so that it can itself be folded or proved succinctly. Both are outside the
         scope of this paper; cf. item (7) of §5.
    (10) Relatedly, ΠLBF is not a folding scheme in the usual instance-in/instance-out sense. Its input relation
         is the uncommitted, unpadded CCS◦ (b) of Definition 3.28, so c1 is a prover message rather than an
         instance component (Remark 3.27); this is exactly what makes the zero-knowledge reading available,
         and exactly what breaks the type match with a folding step. Protocol 13 is a blueprint, its recursive
         branch is not proved, and by Remark 4.8 the extraction survives only O(1) rounds in any case. The
         object we prove things about is a one-shot wrapper for a finished circuit, which is the use case of
         §1.2.1.
1.4. Paper organization. The paper is organized as follows. §2 recalls the interactive-reduction frame-
work of SuperNeo, the notion of a blinding folding reduction, strong sampling sets, and the two commitment
schemes we rely on (the succinct Ajtai scheme and the hiding ABDLOP scheme [BDL+ 16, LNP22]), and
generalizes the interactive-reduction formalism to allow completeness errors. §3 develops the components
that make SuperNeo’s Sum-Check zero-knowledge: Libra-style masking, the componentwise instantiation
of ABDLOP over RK , the companion procedures from the ABDLOP proofs of knowledge, and the rejec-
tion sampling recalled in §3.6. §4 assembles the three modified reductions Π′R1CS , Π′RLC and Π′DEC into the
LatticeBlindFold protocol and proves it complete, knowledge-sound, and blinding, with consolidated error
expressions in Propositions 4.15 and 4.17. §4.3.3 collects the complexity counts, and §4.4 addresses a Corol-
lary 4.24, an accumulator-free variant of ΠLBF . §5 discusses open questions and follow-up work. Deferred
proofs are collected in §B.

                                       2. Recalls and preliminaries
    We collect in this section recalls from SuperNeo [NS26] as well as all the necessary technical background.
2.1. Notation and Global Parameters. We consolidate here the notation and conventions used through-
out this work, expanding upon the baseline established in [NS26].

• Asymptotics and sets. λ denotes the security parameter, fixed to λ = 120 throughout. We write
   negl(λ) and poly(λ) for a negligible and a polynomial function of λ, PPT and EPT for probabilistic
   polynomial time and expected probabilistic polynomial time, and [n] := {1, . . . , n}. All asymptotics
   implicitly depend on λ.
• Polynomials and evaluations. Fd [X1 , . . . , Xn ] is the ring of multivariate polynomials over F in n

   is unconstrained, and ZSℓ ⊆ F[X1 , . . . , Xℓ ] is the set of polynomials vanishing on the Boolean

• Rings, fields and dimensions. F is a finite field of prime order q and K a low-degree extension of
   F with 1/|K| = negl(λ), so that F is naturally a subfield of K. We let Φ(X) := X d + Φd−1 X d−1 +
   · · · + Φ0 ∈ F[X] be the η-th cyclotomic polynomial, of degree d, and set RF := F[X]/(Φ(X)) and
   RK := K[X]/(Φ(X)), the former embedding naturally into the latter; we fix the ring automorphism
   σ : X 7→ X −1 ; in this note we focus on the case Φ(X) = X d + 1, i.e. η = 2d and d is a power of 2.
   We write m for the number of constraints, nR for the length of a ring vector and nF = d · nR for
   the corresponding field vector length, with nR,in and nF,in the respective input lengths; u denotes a
   polynomial degree bound, t the number of structural matrices, k, K ∈ Z≥1 the numbers of instances
   to be folded, and b, B ∈ Z≥2 , with B = bk < q/2, the norm bounds. We write nF,bl for the length
   of the blinding block, the trailing segment of the witness whose sole role is to randomize the Ajtai
   image L(z) (Definition 3.1); we take d | nF,bl and set nR,bl := nF,bl /d, so that the block occupies
                                                                            κ×n
   whole ring elements and the corresponding column block Abl ∈ RF R,bl of the commitment matrix
   is well defined. Both nF,in and nF,bl are counted inside nF .
• Commitments and salts. κ and κ′ denote the module ranks of the Ajtai and of the BDLOP com-
   ponents of the ABDLOP commitment scheme (§2.5), and κcom := κ + κ′ the total number of ring
   components of an ABDLOP commitment Cj ∈ RκF com (cf. §2.5.2). The BDLOP rank is by definition
   the number of message slots the commitment carries, so κ′ = ℓ, and we use the two interchangeably.

Notation 2.1. A warning on overloaded symbols, since several are inherited from the two sources we build
on and cannot be renamed without breaking the correspondence with them. The letter ℓ is used for the number
of Sum-Check variables log2 (m), for the BDLOP message length of §2.5.2, for the coefficient index in cf(·)ℓ ,
and for the challenge-vector dimension of Lemma 2.19; l, separately, is the number of irreducible factors of
X d + 1 modulo q. The letter s denotes a structure, s a Gaussian width, and s1 , s2 the commitment salts.
The letter m is the number of constraints, and in the ABDLOP literature also the committed message, which
is why Lemma 2.31 renames the latter to y. Finally κ is the Ajtai rank here and the challenge coefficient
bound in [LNP22]. In each case the intended meaning is fixed by the surrounding subsection, and we flag the
ambiguity at the point of use where it could plausibly bite.
   The parameters above, together with the objects fixed below, are what protocol statements later refer to
as the global parameters.
Definition 2.2 (Global parameters). In addition to F, K, d, RF , RK , m, nF , nR , nF,in , nR,in , u, t, k, K, b, B fixed
above, we also fix:
     • A structure s, formally defined in §2.4.1 below as a collection of structural matrices together with a
        degree-u polynomial.
     • A strong sampling set C ⊆ RF (Definition 2.13 below) with expansion factor T , such that (K +
        k)T (b − 1) < B and 1/|C| = negl(λ).
     • A ring commitment scheme com := (Setup, Commit) (Definition 2.27 below), homomorphic and
        (2B, C)-relaxed binding. For pp ← Setup(1λ , m), we write L := Commit(pp, ·) : RnF R → C for the
        induced RF -module homomorphism. We additionally require L to be hiding on the blinding block in
        the sense of Lemma 3.3. (This is a condition on the witness layout of Definition 3.1 and on nR,bl ,
        not on the commitment scheme, which remains the plain compact Ajtai scheme of §2.5.1.)
                                                             n
     • The trivial RF -module homomorphism Lin : RnF R → RF R,in , projecting onto the first nR,in columns.
2.2. Interactive reductions. We recall here the concept of interactive reductions, which generalize tradi-
tional reductions to an interactive setting between a prover and a verifier. The following definitions adapt
the framework introduced in [NS26, §4,6] to our setting and extend the notion of blinding to interactive
reductions.
Definition 2.3 (Interactive Reductions, [NS26, Definition 9]). An interactive reduction from relation R1 to
relation R2 (over parameters, structure, instance, and witness tuples) is defined by a tuple of PPT algorithms
(G, K, P, V):
       • G(1λ , sz) → pp: Generates public parameters pp given a security parameter λ and size parameters
          sz.
       • K(pp, s) → (pk, vk): Deterministically outputs a prover key pk and verifier key vk derived from pp
          and a given structure s.
       • P(pk, u1 , w1 ) → (u2 , w2 ): Interactively reduces the check of (pp, s, u1 , w1 ) ∈ R1 into a new claim
          (pp, s, u2 , w2 ) ∈ R2 .
       • V(vk, u1 ) → u2 : Interactively reduces instance u1 ∈ R1 into a new instance u2 ∈ R2 .
The complete execution of the protocol is denoted ⟨P, V⟩((pk, vk), u1 , w1 ), which ultimately outputs the ver-
ifier’s new instance u2 and the prover’s corresponding witness w2 ; we treat ⟨P, V⟩ as a function that takes
as input ((pk, vk), u1 , w1 ) and runs the interaction on the prover’s input (pk, u1 , w1 ) and the verifier’s input
(vk, u1 ).
Definition 2.4 (Reduction of Knowledge (RoK), [NS26, Definition 9]). An interactive reduction (G, K, P, V)
is a Reduction of Knowledge (RoK) if it achieves:
                                                LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                              11


   (1) Completeness (with error εc ): For any EPT adversary A, given pp ← G(1λ , sz), (s, u1 , w1 ) ←
       A(pp) such that (pp, s, u1 , w1 ) ∈ R1 , we have that, except with probability at most εc = εc (λ) over
       the honest parties’ coins, the prover’s output instance is equal to the verifier’s output instance u2 ,
       and
                                     (pp, s, ⟨P, V⟩((pk, vk), u1 , w1 )) ∈ R2 .
       We call the reduction perfectly complete when εc = 0 and statistically complete when εc = negl(λ),
       and we say that it is εc -complete to record the error explicitly.
   (2) Knowledge soundness: For any EPT adversary (A, P ∗ ), there exists an EPT extractor E such
       that if the success probability of the adversary

   (3) Public Coin: All of the verifier’s messages are uniformly random strings of some prescribed length.
       Furthermore, the verifier’s messages contain all of the random coins (randomness) used by the veri-
       fier.
Remark 2.5. SuperNeo’s reductions are perfectly complete, and [NS26, Definition 9] accordingly states
completeness without an error term. Ours cannot be, by construction. Let us notice that completeness errors
accumulate additively under composition (cf. Proposition 2.8).
   We make use of two conventions:
   (1) The loops are capped. An uncapped loop is complete with error 0 but only expected-polynomial-time,
       which breaks the extraction arguments (Remark B.2) and, in the interactive setting, is not enforceable
       by V. We cap every loop at Wmax for Protocol 7 and charge the resulting exhaustion probability to
       εc . The two errors are of the same shape, (1 − 1/M )W ≤ 2−λ , but arise from unrelated loops and
       must be counted separately.
   (2) The failure event is public and witness-independent. Budget exhaustion is an abort declared by the
       honest prover; it is not a verifier rejection of a valid statement, and, by Lemma 3.20, its probability
       is (up to statistical distance 2−λ ) independent of the witness being folded. This is what lets the
       completeness error stay outside the blinding analysis: a simulator reproduces the abort pattern by
       tossing coins with the same, publicly known, bias, without knowing the witness (cf. Remark 2.12).
   To build composite reductions, we rely on the notions of weak and strong reductions. We fix relations
R1 , R′1 , R2 , and R′2 over public parameters, structure, instance, and witness tuples such that R1 ⊆ R′1 and
R2 ⊆ R′2 . Let Ui and Wi be, respectively, the ambient instance and witness spaces of Ri , i ∈ [2].
   Both notions below are stated relative to a pair of functions: an instance projection ϕ, as in [NS26,
Definitions 16–17], and a witness projection ψ, which is new here. In loc. cit. the two agreement conditions
are stated as equality of the extracted witnesses, which is legitimate there because a CE witness is the vector
z alone, and z is determined by ϕ(u) = (ci )i through the (relaxed) binding of L. Our witnesses carry two
further components, the evaluation hints {yi,j } and the commitment salts {si,j }, and neither is a function
of ϕ(u). The hints depend on the Sum-Check point r′ , which two runs of Π′R1CS do not share, and the salts
are resampled afresh at every commitment. Verbatim witness equality therefore fails for essentially every
pair of runs, and with it the second clause of Definition 2.6 and the hypothesis of Definition 2.7.(2). We
accordingly ask for agreement only under ψ, which is all the extraction arguments consume; taking ψ = id
recovers [NS26, Definitions 16–17] exactly.
Definition 2.6 (Weak Interactive Reductions; cf. [NS26, Definition 16]). An interactive reduction Π : R1 →
R2 , defined by PPT algorithms (G, K, P, V), is weak (with completeness error εc ) if it is εc -complete, public
coin, and there exist functions ϕ : U1 → C and ψ : W1 → D (for arbitrary spaces C, D) such that for any

Proposition 2.8 (Strong-Weak Composition, [NS26, Theorem 12]). Consider relations R1 , R2 , R′2 and R3
over public parameters, structure, instance, and witness tuples such that R2 ⊆ R′2 . Let U2 and W2 be the
ambient instance and witness spaces of R2 . Consider interactive reductions Π1 : R1 → R2 (R′2 ), Π2 : R2
(R′2 ) → R3 such that
    (1) Π1 is strong with respect to a pair of functions ϕ : U2 → C, ψ : W2 → D and
    (2) Π2 is weak with respect to the same pair (ϕ, ψ),
                                                 LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                         13


then the sequential composition Π2 ◦ Π1 : R1 → R3 is a reduction of knowledge. Moreover, if Π1 and Π2
                          (1)      (2)                                                        (1)  (2)
have completeness errors εc and εc respectively, then Π2 ◦ Π1 has completeness error at most εc + εc .
The completeness clause is not part of [NS26, Theorem 12], whose reductions are perfectly complete, but
it requires no new argument. The composed honest execution runs Π1 and then Π2 on Π1 ’s output, so it
fails only if one of the two does, and a union bound applies. We record the clause explicitly because, unlike
in loc. cit., in our setting both summands are nonzero. The ψ-clause requires no change to the argument
of [NS26, Theorem 12] either. That proof uses witness agreement in exactly one place, namely to discharge
the hypothesis of Definition 2.7.(2) for Π1 from the conclusion of Definition 2.6 for Π2 ; since both are now
stated under the same ψ, the implication chains verbatim. It is for this reason that Π1 and Π2 must agree
on ψ and not merely on ϕ.
Proposition 2.9 (Sequential composition of reductions of knowledge, [KP22, Theorem 5]). If Π1 : R1 → R2
and Π2 : R2 → R3 are reductions of knowledge with knowledge-soundness errors ε1 , ε2 and completeness
        (1) (2)                                                                              (1)  (2)
errors εc , εc , then Π2 ◦ Π1 : R1 → R3 is a reduction of knowledge with errors ε1 + ε2 and εc + εc .
The completeness clause is again not in loc. cit., whose reductions are perfectly complete, and is the same
union bound recorded above. We use Proposition 2.8 for the inner composition Π′RLC ◦ Π′R1CS , which is
genuinely weak-after-strong, and Proposition 2.9 for the outer one with Π′DEC , both factors being reductions
of knowledge outright.
Definition 2.10 (Folding scheme). A folding scheme for a relation R is defined as a reduction of knowledge
mapping R × RACC → RACC for an accumulator relation RACC .
   We write now the definition of blinding interactive reduction. By abuse of notation, we will refer to an
interactive reduction of the form ΠF old : R1 → R2 , for R1 = Rl × R′ (for any l ≥ 1), as a folding interactive
relation.
Definition 2.11 (Blinding Folding Interactive Reduction). A folding interactive reduction Π : R1 = Rl ×
R′ → R2 , more precisely Π = (G, K, P, V) for (R1 , R2 , s, λ), is blinding if there exists a sampling algorithm
sampleR for instance-witness pairs in R such that for any honest-but-curious verifier V ∗ (modeled as a
                                                                                            $
deterministic algorithm V ∗ (transcript; ω) taking as auxiliary input a coin string ω ← Ω sampled uniformly at
random and independently of P, from which every protocol challenge is deterministically derived, by a fixed
public map whose pushforward of the uniform distribution on Ω is the honest challenge distribution of Π, so
that V ∗ may compute its challenges early but not bias them), there exists an EPT simulator S for which the
following holds.
For any PPT adversary A given
      • pp ← G(1λ , sz) and ((u1 , w1 ), (u2 , w2 )) ← A(pp) such that (pp, s, u1 , w1 ) ∈ Rl−1 , and (pp, s, u2 , w2 ) ∈
          R′ , and
      • for (pk, vk) ← K(pp, s),
we have
   (                                                                                    )
                           $
     (st, w)   (u r , wr ) ← sample R (pp, s),                                            ∼
                                                                                          =
               (st, (u, w)) ← ⟨P, V ∗ ⟩((pk, vk), ((ur , u1 ), u2 ), ((wr , w1 ), w2 ))
                                                                            {(st, w) | (st, w) ← S(pp, s, (u1 , u2 ))},
where st denotes the (arbitrary) output of V ∗ .
As in [KS23, Definition 14], V ∗ is deterministic given its randomness, exactly as in the public-coin setting
of §2; this is what lets the simulator, which does not have oracle access to V ∗ ’s internal coins, reproduce an
identically-distributed view by sampling fresh coins of its own and running the same deterministic map on
them. Clearly, for a (multi-)folding scheme, we recover the definition of blinding in [KS23, Definition 14].
   In the setting of this paper, l = 1, R′ = CCS(b, L) (Definition 2.21), and R = R2 = CEcom (b, L, B̃, Commit)k ,
(Definitions 3.21).
Remark 2.12 (Aborts and blinding). Since ⟨P, V ∗ ⟩ may now abort (Remark 2.5), the left-hand distribution
of Definition 2.11 is over all executions, aborting ones included, and S must reproduce it as such rather than
only its non-aborting part. This costs nothing, for two reasons
14                                            LATTICEBLINDFOLD


      • First, the abort pattern is simulatable on its own. Whether a given attempt of any of our rejection
        loops accepts is, by Lemma 3.20 (resp. its ABDLOP analogue), a coin of publicly known bias 1/M
        (resp. 1/MPoK ) that is within statistical distance 2−λ of being independent of the secret. S tosses its
        own coins with that bias, and aborts where they say to.
      • Second, an aborted attempt is not discarded from V ∗ ’s view in the interactive setting. It leaves behind
        whatever the prover already sent in that attempt. This is exactly why the mask’s Ajtai image cy is
        committed under ABDLOP at Step 6 of Protocol 7 and opened only on acceptance (Remark B.9),
        and why the ABDLOP proofs retransmit only a fresh (w, v) on a retry, never a fresh commitment.
        A rejected round therefore carries hiding commitments and nothing else, and the hybrid argument of
        Theorem 4.13 applies to it verbatim.
In the end, under Fiat–Shamir the second point is no more meaningful (at the level of completeness), as
aborted attempts are simply never output. We stress that this removes the blinding cost of aborts, not the
completeness error itself. The latter disappears only if the attempt caps are lifted as well, in which case the
honest prover becomes expected polynomial time rather than strictly PPT. That is harmless non-interactively,
as there is no verifier to enforce a cap, and the prover simply hashes again, but it is a change of prover model,
not a free consequence of Fiat–Shamir; cf. Remark B.2.
2.3. Strong Sampling Sets: Controlling Norm Growth in Folding. In folding           P schemes, provers and
verifiers linearly combine instances using random coefficients ρi , such that zout = i ρi zi . If these challenges
are sampled from the entire ring RF , the resulting norms can explode exponentially, compromising the
underlying MSIS hardness. To prevent this, we restrict the challenges to a strong sampling set.
Definition 2.13 ((Strong sampling sets, [NS26, Definition 6])). A subset C ⊆ RF is a strong sampling set if
it satisfies two conditions:
     (1) For any distinct elements a, b ∈ C, the difference a − b has an infinity norm strictly less than the
         low-norm invertibility threshold binv (Proposition 2.14), guaranteeing that a − b is invertible over
         RF .
     (2) Its expansion factor, defined as T (C) := maxv∈RF ,ρ∈C ∥ρv∥ ∞
                                                                 ∥v∥∞ , is bounded by O(ϕ(η) · maxρ∈C ∥ρ∥∞ ).


                                             p Theorem 1.1, Conjecture 2.6]). Let z divide η, with q ≡ 1
Proposition 2.14 (Low norm invertibility [LS17,
(mod z) and ordη (q) = η/z. Define binv := 1/ τ (z) · q 1/ϕ(z) (where τ (z) = z for odd z, or z/2 for even z).
Any non-zero element a ∈ RF satisfying ∥a∥∞ < binv is guaranteed to be invertible.
Remark 2.15. Note that, since Definition 2.13 bounds ∥a − b∥∞ < binv for distinct a, b ∈ C, and since in
our instantiations every ρ ∈ C itself satisfies ∥ρ∥∞ < binv , (as 0 ∈ C or not, this is the same small-coefficient
bound used to construct C), Proposition 2.14 applies directly to any single ρ ∈ C − {0}, that is, every nonzero
element of C is invertible in RF .
   By establishing an expansion factor, we ensure that multiplying any vector v by a challenge ρ ∈ C safely
bounds the output norm as ∥ρv∥∞ ≤ T (C) · ∥v∥∞ . When folding K + k witnesses, the norm of the combined
witness is bounded by (K + k) · T (C) times the maximum norm of the witnesses.
Proposition 2.16 (Expansion factors, [AL21]). For any strong sampling set C over the cyclotomic ring RF ,
the expansion factor T (C) is strictly bounded by 2 · ϕ(η) · maxρ∈C ∥ρ∥∞ .
   In practice, C is built using ring elements with coefficients drawn from a small interval [−β, β]. This gives
expansion factor T (C) ≈ O(ϕ(η) · β), a modest constant, allowing the protocol to execute folding steps safely
without norm blowup.
Remark 2.17 (The factor 2 is not needed for power-of-two cyclotomics). Proposition 2.16 is stated for
a general cyclotomic ring, and the factor 2 there accounts for the reduction modulo a general Φ. Under
restriction (6) of §1.3 we work throughout with Φ(X) = X d + 1, d a power of two, and there the elementary
bound is tight without it: multiplication in RF is a negacyclic convolution, so for any ρ, v
                                      ∥ρv∥∞ ≤ ∥ρ∥1 ∥v∥∞ ≤ d β ∥v∥∞ ,
whence T (C) ≤ ϕ(η)β.
                                                   LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                          15

if and only if xi ̸= yi but xj = yj for all j ̸= i. A special set SS(C, ℓ) is defined as:
                             SS(C, ℓ) = (⃗c, ⃗c1 , . . . , ⃗cℓ ) ∈ (C ℓ )ℓ+1 : ∀i ∈ [ℓ], ⃗c ≡i ⃗ci .

2.4. Recalls of SuperNeo. We recall here the SuperNeo [NS26] protocol. The protocol consists of a
composition of reductions of knowledge ΠSuperNeo := ΠDEC ◦ ΠRLC ◦ ΠCCS , built on the global parameters
(the fields, rings, and dimensions, the strong sampling set C, the ring commitment scheme com, and the
morphisms L, Lin ) fixed once and for all in Definition 2.2 (§2.1).
   The three reductions ΠCCS , ΠRLC , ΠDEC are not our contribution, and none of them are modified in this
subsection: we recall their relations and resulting properties in prose below, and refer the reader to [NS26,
§§7.3–7.5] for the full protocol statements. Our own modifications to each of these three reductions, which
constitute the technical core of the paper, are introduced later in §4; the reader already familiar with [NS26]
can skip directly to §4 and use [NS26, §7] only as a lookup reference.
2.4.1. The embedding and the relations. As in [NS26, §7.1], we define a structure as a collection of

Lemma 2.20 (Degree-0 ring elements multiply as scalars). Let â, b̂ ∈ RK both be degree-0, i.e. cf(â)ℓ =
cf(b̂)ℓ = 0 for all ℓ ≥ 2 (equivalently â = ct(â) and b̂ = ct(b̂) as constant polynomials in RK ). Then â · b̂ ∈ RK
is itself degree-0, and
                                               ct(â · b̂) = ct(â) · ct(b̂).
                                                                                               Qr
More generally, for any finite product â1 · · · âr of degree-0 elements, ct(â1 · · · âr ) = i=1 ct(âi ).

Proof. Write â = ct(â) · 1 and b̂ = ct(b̂) · 1 as elements of RK = K[X]/Φ(X), i.e. constant polynomials.
Multiplication in RK restricted to the constant subring K · 1 ⊂ RK agrees with multiplication in K, since 1
is the multiplicative identity and Φ(X) has degree d ≥ 1, so no reduction modulo Φ(X) is triggered by a
product of two degree-0 polynomials. Hence â · b̂ = (ct(â) · ct(b̂)) · 1, which is again degree-0 with constant
term ct(â) · ct(b̂). The general claim follows by induction on r.                                             ■

   The two main relations SuperNeo handles are the Norm-bounded CCS relation and the Norm-bounded
CCS Evaluation relation. We note that [NS26] states these relations over a native relation field L = Fqτ
embedded, together with K, into a common ambient field A, with the witness packed into RnF R via FlatL ,
PackL and TransL ([NS26, §5]). We work throughout at τ = 1, i.e. L = F and A = K, as in all three
parameterizations of [NS26, §8], and state the relations over F accordingly; see [NS26, Definitions 19–20] for
the general form.

Remark 2.23. The morphism L is taken to be a ring commitment scheme (see §2.5) Commit(pi, •); recall
Definition 2.2 and cf. [NS26, Definition 21].
2.4.2. The interactive reductions. The full protocol statements may be found in [NS26, §§7.3–7.5]; we
record here only a brief account of them and the properties we rely on.
2.4.2.1. CCS Reduction – ΠCCS . The main interactive reduction we need to address in order to define a
suitable LatticeBlindFold protocol is the RoK ΠCCS . Given K fresh CCS instances and k prior evaluation
claims, it checks (simultaneously) that the K CCS instances satisfy their constraints, that the k evaluation
claims hold at the carried-over point r, and that the norms of all K + k witness vectors involved are bounded
by b; the output is a fresh batch of K + k evaluation claims at a new point r′ .
   It achieves this via a single invocation of the Sum-Check protocol on a composite polynomial
(2.3)                  ⃗ := eq(X,
                     Q(X)      ⃗ α) · (F(X)
                                         ⃗ + γ K · NC(X))
                                                      ⃗ + γ 2K+k · Eval(X)
                                                                        ⃗ ∈ K[X],
                                                                              ⃗
                                              LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                   17


where F(X)⃗ encodes the K CCS constraints, NC(X)    ⃗ encodes the K + k norm constraints, Eval(X)   ⃗ encodes
the k carried-over evaluation claims, and α, γ are verifier challenges batching these three families of checks
                                                           ′      ]        ′
into one sum. The prover sends fresh evaluation hints yi,j    := M̄ j zi (r ) at the end of the Sum-Check, and
the verifier checks a single evaluation identity combining them.
Lemma 2.24 (ΠCCS is strong, [NS26, Lemma 7, Appendix B.2]). The interactive reduction ΠCCS : CCS(b, L)K ×
CE(b, L)k → CE(b, L)K+k is strong for the function ϕ, which projects commitments (ci )i∈[K+k] from the in-
stance.
2.4.2.2. Random linear combination reduction – ΠRLC . The interactive reduction ΠRLC performs the ex-
plicit folding step: given K + k input CCS evaluation claims of norm b, the verifier samples challenges
ρ1 , . . . , ρK+k ← C from the strong sampling set (Definition 2.13), and the prover and verifier jointly com-
pute the corresponding random linear combination of commitments, public inputs, and evaluation hints,
outputting a single claim of larger norm B = bk .
Lemma 2.25 (ΠRLC is weak, [NS26, Lemma 8, Lemma 11, Appendix B.3]). The interactive reduction
ΠRLC : CE(b, L)K+k → CE(B, L) is weak for the function ϕ, which projects commitments (ci )i∈[K+k] from
the instance.
2.4.2.3. Decomposition reduction – ΠDEC . The final reduction reduces the norm of claims from B = bk
back down to b, so that CCS claims can be folded repeatedly without the norm of the witness openings
growing without bound. The prover computes a b-ary decomposition splitb (z) = (z1 , . . . , zk ) of the witness
(Definition 2.2), together with matching decompositions of the commitment and evaluation hints, and the
verifier checks that these recombine correctly.
Proposition 2.26 (ΠDEC is a RoK, [NS26, Theorem 13]). ΠDEC : CE(B, L) → CE(b, L)k is a reduction of
knowledge.
2.5. Ring commitments. We consider several commitment schemes, all over rings.
Definition 2.27 (Ring Commitment Scheme, [NS26, Definition 7]). A ring commitment scheme com :=
(Setup, Commit) consists of two PPT algorithms:
     • Setup(1λ , m) → pp: Takes as input a security parameter 1λ and length m ∈ Z≥1 , outputs public
        parameters pp.
     • Commit(pp, z) → c: Takes as input public parameters pp and a vector z ∈ RFm , outputs a commitment
        c ∈ C.
A ring commitment scheme can satisfy the following properties:
     • B-binding: For every length m = poly(λ) and every EPT adversary A, a ring commitment scheme
        is B-binding (for B ∈ Z≥1 ) if the following probability holds:
                                                                             
                     Commit(pp, z1 ) = Commit(pp, z2 )                  λ
                                                         pp ← Setup(1 , nR ) 
                 Pr  ∧ ∥z1 ∥∞ , ∥z2 ∥∞ < B,                                   ≤ ϵbind (B)
                    
                                                         z1 , z2 ∈ RFm ← A(pp)
                      ∧ z1 ̸= z2
       for ϵbind (B) ≤ negl(λ). We refer to a pair of vectors (z1 , z2 ) which satisfies the conditions in the
       probability as a B-binding collision.
     • (B, C)-relaxed binding: For every length m = poly(λ) and every EPT adversary A, a ring commitment
       scheme is (B, C)-relaxed binding (for B ∈ Z≥1 and set C ⊆ RF ) if the following probability holds:
                   ∆1 · c = Commit(pp, z1 )
                                                                               
                                                pp ← Setup(1λ , m)
                   ∧ ∆2 · c = Commit(pp, z2 )                                
               Pr 
                                                       c ∈ C,                   ≤ ϵrlx (B, C)
                                                                                
                   ∧ ∥z1 ∥∞ , ∥z2 ∥∞ < B,      ∆1 , ∆2 ∈ (C − C), ← A(pp)
                    ∧ ∆1 z2 ̸= ∆2 z1                z1 , z2 ∈ RFm
       for ϵrlx (B, C) ≤ negl(λ). We refer to a tuple of elements (c, ∆1 , ∆2 , z1 , z2 ) which satisfies the condi-
       tions in the probability as a (B, C)-relaxed binding collision.
     • Homomorphic: For every m ∈ Z≥1 and pp ∈ Setup(1λ , m), the commitment algorithm, Commit(pp, ·) :
       RFm → C, is an RF -module homomorphism.

2.5.1. Ajtai Commitment Schemes. We begin by introducing the Ajtai commitment scheme, which
serves as the foundational, succinct, lattice-based commitment protocol used throughout our folding con-
structions. Relying on the Module Short Integer Solution (MSIS) assumption, the Ajtai scheme provides
binding, homomorphic commitments for norm-bounded ring elements.
Definition 2.28 (Compact Ajtai commitment scheme). Let the message length be m ∈ Z≥1 and let κ ∈ Z≥1
denote the commitment rank. The Ajtai commitment scheme comAjtai := (Setup, Commit) over a ring RF
consists of two PPT algorithms:
                                                                                 $
        • Setup(1λ , m, κ) → pp: Samples a uniformly random matrix A ←  − Rκ×m
                                                                            F  . Outputs public parameters
          pp := A.
        • Commit(pp, z) → c: Given pp and a vector z ∈ Rm                                        κ
                                                         F , outputs the commitment c := Az ∈ RF .

Remark 2.29. A critical property of comAjtai is that it is perfectly homomorphic over RF : Commit(pp, z1 +
αz2 ) = Commit(pp, z1 ) + αCommit(pp, z2 ). Note, however, that standard Ajtai commitments (as well as
the ones considered in [NS26]) are not hiding. For components of our protocol requiring zero-knowledge
(e.g., hiding witnesses or evaluation hints), we will later introduce the computationally hiding ABDLOP
commitment schemes (see §2.5.2).
Proposition 2.30 ([NS26, Theorem 6]). The Ajtai commitment scheme is a ring commitment scheme that
is homomorphic B-binding (assuming the MSIS∞,κ,q
                                              m,2B problem is hard), and (B, C)-relaxed binding (assuming
the MSIS∞,κ,q
        m,4T B problem is hard) and C is a strong sampling set with expansion factor T .

   Together with the property of Lemma 2.31, a key motivation for the adoption of the ABDLOP commitment

2.6. Security assumptions. The underlying security of our folding procedure fundamentally relies on well-
established lattice hardness assumptions defined over cyclotomic rings. Let R denote a cyclotomic ring, and
let Rq = R/qR for a chosen modulus q. The central assumption required for knowledge soundness in this
context is the Module Short Integer Solution (MSIS) problem [LS15, BDL+ 16, LNP22].
Definition 2.32 (Module-SIS (MSISn,m,q,B )). Given a uniformly random matrix A ← Rn×m
                                                                                   q  , the MSISn,m,q,B
problem asks to find a non-zero vector z ∈ Rm such that Az = 0 (mod q) and ∥z∥ ≤ B.
   Unless subscripted, ∥ · ∥ denotes the ℓ∞ -norm fixed earlier in this section, extended coefficient-wise to
vectors and matrices of ring elements.
   As observed in [NS26] and [BC24], strictly bounding the norm B across sequential operations is critical.
If the norms grow too rapidly during folding, the scheme becomes vulnerable to trivial attacks or requires
impractically large parameters to preserve the MSIS assumption.
Definition 2.33 (Module-LWE, decisional (MLWEn,m,q,χ )). Let χ be a distribution over R (concretely,
throughout this paper, the uniform distribution over {c ∈ R : ∥c∥∞ ≤ ν} for a bound ν). Given a uniformly
random matrix A ← Rn×m  q   and a secret s ← χm , the MLWEn,m,q,χ problem asks to distinguish (A, As mod
                          n
q) from (A, u) for u ← Rq uniform, with non-negligible advantage.

Remark 2.36 (Security of the constructions recalled above). Every hardness assumption used in this pa-
per reduces to MSIS (Definition 2.32) or MLWE (Definitions 2.33–2.34) over RF (= R), both believed to
resist quantum adversaries: under MSIS the Ajtai and CommitBDLOP /CommitABDLOP schemes of §2.5
are (relaxed-)binding, and under MLWE the latter two are additionally hiding, with honest-verifier zero-
knowledge proofs of knowledge.
   In the end, as in [LNP22, LNS20], we assume that Extended-MLWE is almost as hard as plain MLWE.
Similarly, we do assume that the hardness of DKS∞
                                                n,k,β is the same of MLWE at secret dimension k − n with
n samples.

                                        3. The zk-fying components
    Having recalled in §2 the interactive-reduction framework, the notion of a blinding folding reduction, and
the commitment and hardness assumptions we rely on, we now turn to the technical core of the paper, that
is, making SuperNeo’s Sum-Check reduction zero-knowledge, one obstruction at a time.

3.1. Hiding for Ajtai commitments. The one place where an Ajtai image must nevertheless be hiding
is the input instance c1 = L(z1 ), which no ABDLOP layer can wrap without destroying succinctness of
the folded instance. We handle it by randomizing the preimage instead via Lemma 3.3. We make use of
Definition 3.1.
Definition 3.1 (Blinded R1CS layout). Fix nF,bl as in §2.1 and let Ibl := {nF − nF,bl + 1, . . . , nF }. We say
that a structure s = (M1 , . . . , Mt , f ) and a witness follow the blinded layout if
    (1) z = [x, w] exactly as in Definition 2.21, with the last nF,bl coordinates of w, indexed by Ibl , designated
        as the blinding block e and sampled uniformly from {v : ∥v∥∞ < b} by the honest prover.
    (2) some fixed index ι1 ≤ nF,in carries the constant-one wire, zι1 = 1;
    (3) for every i ∈ Ibl , (M1 )i,• = δi , (M2 )i,• = δi and (M3 )i,• = δι1 , where δj is the j-th standard basis
        row;
    (4) for every i ∈
                    / Ibl and every j ∈ [t], (Mj )i,Ibl = 0: the circuit rows have no support on the blinding
        columns.
Clause (4) holds automatically when the layout is reached by appending fresh variables and fresh rows to
an existing structure, which is how it arises in practice. We state it because the truncation of Lemma 3.29
(see below) depends on it, and clauses (1)–(3) alone do not imply it. Note that it constrains the constraint
matrices only: the commitment L does have support on Ibl , and must, since Abl e is what Lemma 3.3 relies
on.
Remark 3.2. Under Definition 3.1, row i ∈ Ibl of the R1CS predicate f (Y1 , Y2 , Y3 ) = Y2 Y3 − Y1 reads
zi · 1 − zi = 0, which holds for every value of zi . The block is therefore unconstrained by s, and the language
decided by CCS(b, L) is unchanged. A circuit is satisfiable for public input x under the blinded layout iff it is
under the original one.
    Now, it follows that we can still assume that M1 = Im .,NC(X)    ⃗ = P γ (1) Q (zei − j) ranges over all m
                                                                             i i     j
coordinates, so ∥e∥∞ < b is certified by the existing norm check with no new machinery, and L is the same
matrix, still an RF -module homomorphism, and e travels through Π′RLC ’s rejection sampling and Π′DEC ’s splitb
as ordinary witness coordinates.
Lemma 3.3 (L is hiding under the blinded layout). Write L(z) = Acirc zcirc + Abl e for the column splitting
induced by Ibl , and let χb be uniform on {c ∈ RF : ∥c∥∞ ≤ b − 1}. Then for every zcirc , the distribution of
                         n
L([zcirc , e]) over e ← χb R,bl is within εL-hide of uniform on C, in either regime:
     (1) (Statistical.) If the cyclotomic polynomial X d +1 splits into l irreducible factors modulo q, if q ≡ 2l+1
         (mod 4l), and if
                                     q κ/nR,bl · 2256/(nR,bl d) ≤ 2(b − 1) < binv ,
         then εL-hide ≤ 2−128 , statistically and against an unbounded distinguisher. Here κ = κAjtai-Compact .
     (2) (Computational.) Otherwise, if the decisional knapsack problem DKS∞     κ, κ+nR,bl , b is hard, the bound
         holds computationally with εL-hide the corresponding advantage.
                                                   LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                         21


Proof. Acirc zcirc is a fixed shift and the uniform distribution on C is invariant under it, so it suffices that
Abl e be uniform, resp. pseudorandom; Abl is a column block of the uniform Ajtai matrix, hence uniform.
Regime (1) is [BDL+ 16, Lemma 4] applied to Abl , with their ring degree N read as our d, their number
of splitting factors d as our l, and β = b − 1. The upper bound is exactly the invertibility threshold binv
of Proposition 2.14. Regime (2) is [BDL+ 16, Definition 2], after replacing Abl by its Hermite normal form:
fixing the last κ columns and left-multiplying by their inverse is a bijection of Rκq and so preserves any
advantage, and those columns are invertible except with probability at most l/(q d/l − 1). In that form
[ Iκ | A′ ]y = A′ y1 + y2 is normal-form MLWE with secret dimension nR,bl and κ samples, as hard as the
uniform-secret variant by [ACPS09] and [LS15].                                                                ■
   We stress that the layout by itself is not enough. If the security experiment is read over the already-padded
relation, the adversary supplies the blinding block along with the rest of the witness and may set e := 0; the
segment is then as deterministic as everything else and Lemma 3.3 has no hypothesis to work with. What
the layout buys is the possibility of relocating the entropy; what makes it available is reading the experiment
over the unpadded relation of Definition 3.28, where A supplies only (x1 , wcirc ) and e is drawn afterwards by
the prover as part of the embedding (3.19). It is in that reading, and only in it, that the lemma is invoked
below; cf. Remark 3.27.
Remark 3.4 (Binding is untouched). Statistical hiding on the blinding block neither weakens nor in-
teracts with the (B, C)-relaxed binding of Definition 2.27. Unfolding that definition, a relaxed collision
(c, ∆1 , ∆2 , z1 , z2 ) gives L(∆2 z1 − ∆1 z2 ) = ∆2 ∆1 c − ∆1 ∆2 c = 0 with ∆2 z1 − ∆1 z2 ̸= 0 of norm below
4T B, i.e. an MSIS solution. Only the message length changes, MSIS∞,κ,q                 ∞,κ,q
                                                                         nR ,4T B → MSISnR +nR,bl ,4T B . Note that
even though the size is different, the bound B = bk is unchanged, since ∥e∥∞ < b and the block folds as
an ordinary witness segment. What the block does foreclose is a statistically binding instantiation of L,
in the sense available to [BDL+ 16] for their own scheme. However, nothing here or in [NS26] needs it (L
compresses Rnq R onto Rκq ) and the binding was already only computationally binding.
3.2. Companion procedures from the ABDLOP proofs of knowledge. One main feature of the
ABDLOP commitment scheme that we make use of is that it is endowed with zero-knowledge proof of
knowledge of randomness and messages as well as of their linear and quadratic relations. We do not report
them in full and simply recall the main results we need (together with a small modification). We refer the
reader to the algorithms in [LNP22, Fig. 4, Fig. 5] for the linear relations, and to [LNP22, §4, Fig. 6] for
the quadratic-relation proof system. As in loc. cit. we fix Rq = Zq [X]/(X d + 1) to be the cyclotomic ring
modulo q, and let Sβch = {x ∈ Rq : ∥x∥∞ ≤ βch } be the set of polynomials with coefficients bounded by βch ,
which acts as a selection set for the strong sampling set used in these proofs.
Assumption 3.5. For the sake of simplicity, we do not track their completeness errors, as they are in-
tertwined with their rejection parameter; cf. [LNP22, Lemma 2.14, Theorem 4.2, Theorem 4.5, Figure 12].
Instead, we simply assume that they are negligible in the security parameter λABDLOP , and fix them to be
2−λABDLOP ≤ 2−λ .
                            (1)
Lemma 3.6. Protocol Πmany ((s2 , s1 , m), (f1 , f2 , . . . , fN )) [LNP22, Fig. 4] is a proof of knowledge of (s̄1 , s̄2 , c̄) ∈
Rm    m2    ¯
 q × Rq × C satisfying
   1


    (1) A1 s̄1 + A2 s̄√2 = tA ,
    (2) ∥s̄i c̄∥ ⩽ 2si 2mi d for i = 1, 2, and
    (3) R1 s̄1 + Rm (tB − Bs̄2 ) = u.
                                                                       p √                        √
   Under the assumption that MSISn,m1 +m2 ,B is hard for B = 8η (s1 2m1 d)2 + (s2 2m2 d)2 , then
     • the pair (s̄1 , s̄2 ) is unique, and
     • for any two valid transcripts (w, v, c, z1 , z2 ) and (w, v, c′ , z′1 , z′2 ), it holds that zi − cs̄i = z′i − c′ s̄i .
Moreover, the protocol admits a simulator SABDLOP such that, without access to private information s1 , s2 ,
and m, outputs a simulation of a commitment (tA , tB ) along with a non-aborting transcript of the protocol
between prover P and verifier V such that for every algorithm A that has advantage ε in distinguishing the
simulated commitment and transcript from the real commitment and transcript, whenever the prover does
not abort, there is an algorithm A′ with the same running time that has non-negligible (depending on ε)
advantage in distinguishing the Extended-MLWEn+ℓ,1,m2 −n−ℓ,χ,C,s2 .
22                                                LATTICEBLINDFOLD


Proof. The first three statements are [LNP22, Lemma 3.1]. Regarding the simulator SABDLOP , we report
here the explicit hybrid construction contained in the proof of [LNP22, Theorem 4.2]; we refer the reader to
loc. cit. for all the details. Here we only sketch the construction as we will make use of these simulators in
our proofs. Following [LNP22, Theorem 4.2], we build SABDLOP as the last hybrid in a sequence of three
simulators S0 , S1 , and SABDLOP , the first two of which are allowed to use the secret (s1 , m) and are only a
proof device, while the final SABDLOP uses none of it.
Protocol 1 Hybrid S0
     Simulator: S0
     Input: s1 , m

 1: Sample c ← C
 2: Sample s2 ← χm2
 3: Generate (tA , tB ) honestly under fresh randomness.
 4: Sample z1 ← Dsm11                                              ▷ Sample masked responses with rejection
 5: Sample z2 ← Dsm22 conditioned on ⟨s2 , z2 ⟩ ≥ 0      ▷ i.e. run the Rej2 branch of [LNP22, Lemma 2.14].
 6: w := A1 z1 + A2 z2 − c tA                                         ▷ Recompute the commitment openings
 7: v := R1 z1 + Rm (c tB − Bz2 ) − cu
 8: return    (tA , tB ), (w, v, c, z1 , z2 )

By [LNP22, Lemma 2.14] (the Rej2 case), the distribution output by S0 is statistically close to that of an
actual non-aborting execution between the honest prover and the honest verifier.

Note that S1 runs identically to S0 , except that instead of generating (tA , tB ) honestly, it samples u′ ← Rn+ℓ     F  .
Now, we claim that if a PPT adversary A distinguishes the outputs of S0 and S1 with advantage ε, there is a
PPT B solving Extended-MLWEn+ℓ, 1, m2 −n−ℓ,      χ, C, ⋆s2 with  advantage  at least ε/2. Given an Extended-MLWE
tuple (C, u⋆ , z2 , b) for C := AB2 , B sets ttB  := u + Am    1 s1
                                               A
                                                                    
                                                                      and simulates the rest of the transcript identically
to S0 /S1 , then outputs the result to A. Condition on b = 1 (which happens with probability ≥ 1/2): if
u⋆ = Cs2 , the output of B has exactly the distribution of S0 ’s output; if u⋆ is uniform, it has exactly
the distribution of S1 ’s. Hence B’s advantage, conditioned on b = 1, matches A’s, giving unconditional
advantage at least ε/2.

   Simulator: SABDLOP

Combining the two hybrid steps (S0 ≈c S1 under Extended-MLWEn+ℓ, 1, m2 −n−ℓ, χ, C, s2 , and S1 ≡ SABDLOP
as distributions) together with S0 ≈s (real transcript) from [LNP22, Lemma 2.4], the triangle inequality
gives that SABDLOP ’s output is computationally indistinguishable from a real transcript between the honest
prover and honest (possibly malicious-but-passive) verifier.                                             ■
