   To simultaneously prove N quadratic relations f1 , . . . , fN (which is what we need below, in order to batch
the square/cube/product identities into a single sub-protocol call and a single round), the verifier first sends
                                                                      (2)                 PN         

       (2)                                            
this ΠABDLOP,many (s2 , s1 , m), σ, (f1 , . . . , fN ) , cf. [LNP22, Fig. 7]. This adds only a single round of N
                                                          (2)                  
challenge ring elements over the basic protocol ΠABDLOP (s2 , s1 , m), σ, f , and its soundness error degrades
additively by at most q −d/2 (cf. also [LNP22, §4.3]). More precisely, at q ≡ 2l + 1 (mod 4l) with l = 2 the
polynomial X d + 1 has φ(2d)/ord2d (q) = 2 irreducible factors over F, and over the quadratic extension K the
order halves, giving 4 factors of degree d/4. By the Chinese Remainder Theorem RK ∼
                                                                                             Q4
                                                                                           = i=1 K[X]/(fi ) with
each quotient a field of size |K|d/4 = q d/2 . A uniform µ ∈ RK therefore annihilates a fixed nonzero element
with probability at most q −d/2 (from the random linear combination collapsing to zero on a nonzero vector
of relations), which is negligible for our parameter choices.

   A lemma analogous to Lemma 3.7 holds true for the batched version ΠABDLOP,many for N relations.
3.2.1. Vanishing constant coefficients. Several steps of our reduction do not prove a full-ring identity,
but only that the constant coefficient of a committed quantity vanishes.
                (1)
   Protocol Πmany ((s2 , s1 , m), (f1 , f2 , . . . , fN )) proves u = R1 s1 + Rm m for a public u ∈ RN K , i.e. a full-ring
identity. It cannot be invoked directly on a statement about the constant term, since the honest prover’s
function f (s1 , m) is not the zero ring element and the verifier cannot compute its non-constant coefficients.

Lemma 3.8. Protocol 4 is complete, and, under the hypotheses of Lemma 3.6 (resp. Lemma 3.7), is a
proof of knowledge of an opening (s̄1 , s̄2 , m̄) of (tA , tB ) satisfying ct(fj (s̄1 , m̄)) = 0 for every j ∈ [N ], with
                                                     (1)             (2)
knowledge-soundness error exceeding that of Πmany (resp. ΠABDLOP,many ) by an additive 1/|K|. Moreover
it is honest-verifier zero-knowledge. More precisely, h is distributed uniformly on {x ∈ RK : ct(x) = 0},
independently of (s1 , m), so the simulator samples h from that set and invokes SABDLOP on the remaining
transcript.

    Knowledge soundness: The extractor of Lemma 3.6 applied at Step 5 returns an opening with j γj fj (s̄1 , m̄)+

ḡ = h in RK . Taking constant coefficients and using V’s check, j γj ct(fj ) + ct(ḡ) = 0. Both ḡ and the fj ’s

the left-hand side is a nonconstant affine function of γj0 ∈ K, so it vanishes with probability exactly 1/|K|.

onto {x : ct(x) = j γj ct(fj )} = {x : ct(x) = 0}, so h is uniform on that set.                                        ■

Remark 3.9. Over Zq the masking step of [LNP22, Figure 5] has soundness error 1/q and must be repeated
⌈λ/ log2 q⌉ times. Because our challenges are drawn from K = Fq2 with |K| ≈ 2128 , a single execution already
                                                                                                  (1)
gives 1/|K| ≈ 2−128 ≤ 2−λ and thus no repetition is needed. The overhead of Protocol 4 over Πmany is one
committed ring element, one challenge round, and one ring element h sent in the clear.
3.2.1.1. Anchoring salt aggregates to committed messages. In Protocol 6 we must anchor the transmitted

δ0 · cN ) − δ1 · cE (Step 18) to the commitments already in V’s view, so that a cheating prover cannot supply
an arbitrary field value. Note that u⋆ is never transmitted as it is computed by V alone, and h is public (it
is the Sum-Check’s last-round output).

3.2.1.2. Coefficient extraction as a ring multiplication.
Lemma 3.12 (Rotation extraction). Let τ1 := 1 and, for ℓ ∈ {2, . . . , d}, let τℓ := −X d−ℓ+1 ∈ RK . Then for
every a ∈ RK ,
                                               ct(τℓ · a) = cf(a)ℓ .
                  Pd−1
Proof. Write a = i=0 ai X i . Since X d ≡ −1 (mod Φ(X)), multiplying by X d−ℓ+1 sends the coefficient of
X ℓ−1 to the constant slot with a sign flip from the single wrap-around; the extra −1 folded into τℓ exactly
cancels it, and no other coefficient of a contributes to ct(τℓ a) because every other exponent i + d − ℓ + 1
reduces to a nonzero residue mod d (or, for ℓ = 1, no wrap occurs at all).                                 ■

   More generally, for a sum ℓ=1 wℓ cf(a)ℓ with public weights wℓ ∈ K, we define the packaged rotation as

operations, and no fresh commitment is computed beyond the single garbage slot for g. Overall this incurs
O(t · (k + K))× a single ZKPoK.
3.3. Instantiating ABDLOP over RK Componentwise. Throughout this work, the ABDLOP com-
mitment scheme and the accompanying zero-knowledge machinery of [LNP22] are used over a quadratic
extension ring RK = K[X]/(Φ(X)), which by restriction (6) of §1.3 we take throughout to be Φ(X) = X d + 1
with d a power of two, as in [LNP22]. This section specifies precisely how that instantiation is done: rather
than building an ABDLOP commitment whose matrices and messages live natively over RK (which would
require the hardness of MLWE/MSIS over RK itself), we identify K = F[Y ]/(Y 2 − ν) for ν a quadratic
non-residue in F, and commit to the {1, Y }-coordinates of RK -elements directly, as ordinary RF -elements,
and translate every RK -relation used in the protocol into an equivalent pair of RF -relations once, at the level
of public parameters, before invoking [LNP22]’s proof system unchanged.
   Following this approach, the ABDLOP commitment matrices are then genuinely uniform over RF , and
security rests only on standard RF -MLWE/MSIS, the same assumption already used throughout the rest of
the paper, with no additional or non-standard hardness assumption required.
                                                              √
3.3.1. The Rank-Doubling Embedding. Writing K = F( ν) for a fixed non-residue ν ∈ F, every element
                                                           2
of RK decomposes uniquely as a+bY with a, b ∈ RF and Y = ν; recall that RK is a free module of rank 2 over
RF , since RK = RF ⊗F K. This gives an F-algebra isomorphism between RK and its regular representation
acting on R2F ,
                                                                             
                                                                       a νb

   Let us remark that we do not use this decomposition as an embedding of an already-instantiated RK -
scheme, but as the definition of the witness representation itself. More precisely, every (secret or public)
RK -element that appears in the protocol is represented, from the start, as a pair of RF -elements (a, b), and
every commitment is an ordinary ABDLOP commitment to such pairs over RF (cf. §2.5.2, with A1 , A2 , B

coordinate representation of some unique x ∈ RK , and every RK -relation the protocol needs to enforce is
instead enforced directly as a relation on (a, b), built once from ψ at parameter-generation time.
   What still needs to be checked is that this translation preserves
      • the relations the ABDLOP protocols need to prove (linear and quadratic),
      • the norm bounds, and
      • the soundness of K-valued challenge masking.
We address these in the order.

3.3.3.4. Automorphisms. For σi ∈ Aut(RF ) acting only on the ring variable X, we extend it to RK by fixing Y ,
that is, we set σi (a + bY ) = σi (a) + σi (b)Y . In other words, σi commutes with the {1, Y }-decomposition and
acts component-wise on (a, b). This property is independent of the specific quadratic function being expanded
and thus applies uniformly to the sections above. More precisely, the automorphism-stacked quadratic
functions of [LNP22, §§4.1–4.4] (built from (σ i (s1 ))i∈[k] , (σ i (m))i∈[k] ) expand component-wise exactly as
above, with σi simply distributing across the stacking index and the {1, Y }-decomposition independently. In
particular the automorphism-stability machinery of [LNP22, §§4.1–4.4] (the choice of challenge set Cβσch fixed
                                                  LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                 29


under σ, and the trace-based packing trick of [LNP22, Lemma 4.7]) applies verbatim to a and b separately,
with no cross term to track beyond the R̂(a) , R̂(b) bookkeeping already derived.

3.3.4. Norms. Recall that the ABDLOP machinery works with the ℓ2 norm,             p even though we bound the
salt norms in terms of the ℓ∞ one; the conversion is then obtained by ∥x∥2 ≤ |x|∥x∥∞ , for x the length of
x in terms of field elements.
    We define ∥x∥ for x = a + bY ∈ RK to be the coefficient-vector ℓ2 norm of (a, b) in the {1, Y } basis,
i.e. ∥x∥2 := ∥a∥2 + ∥b∥2 . Under this convention φ is an exact isometry, ∥φ(x)∥ = ∥x∥, with no distortion
constant, since {1, Y } is by construction the basis in which φ is coordinate extraction.
    We emphasize that this norm bound is not obtained by instantiating the quadratic-relation translation
of §3.3.3 with fK (x) := σK^(x) · x, the K-level automorphism identity that LNP22’s exact-ℓ2 -norm machinery
(§5.2 of [LNP22]) uses over RF . Extending σ−1 to RK by fixing Y and computing σK (x)x = (σ(a)+σ(b)Y )(a+
                                                                                    2       2         2      2
bY ) = σ(a)a+νσ(b)b + σ(a)b+σ(b)a Y shows that the F-part of σ^          K (x)x is ∥a∥ +ν∥b∥ , not ∥a∥ +∥b∥ –
the two differ by the factor ν on the b-term precisely because ν ̸= 1 (it is a non-residue). Plugging x = a + bY
directly into the K-automorphism identity therefore proves the wrong quantity and must be avoided.
    Instead, ∥a∥2 and ∥b∥2 are each obtained by applying the identity σ−1    ^  (a)a = ∥a∥2 (resp. for b) directly
over RF , using the base-ring automorphism σ−1 ∈ Aut(RF ) already available in [LNP22]; these are two
independent, ordinary RF -quadratic-with-automorphism statements on a and b separately, not an instance
of the RK -relation translation. Consequently any norm bound ∥x∥ ≤ β required by the protocol (approximate
range proof and exact norm proof of [LNP22, §§5.1–5.2]) is proved as the single joint quadratic constraint
∥a∥2 +∥b∥2 ≤ β 2 on the committed (a, b), provable by the same machinery as any other exact-norm statement
in [LNP22, §5.2] with no adaptation beyond summing the two independent square-norm terms; we are only
bounding a norm on a fixed, exact coordinate system, not moving an error distribution across a reduction.

3.3.5. Soundness of K-Valued Challenge Masking. The one place requiring a genuine argument, rather
than routine translation, is the masking step used in [LNP22, §3.2 (Figure 5)] and [LNP22, §4.3 (Figure 8, and
its soundness-boosted version)], where a challenge is used to force a committed value’s constant coefficient to
be zero with soundness error 1/q. In both cases the challenge is drawn as a scalar, γ ∈ Zq (resp. γi,j ∈ Zq for
the boosted version) and not a ring element; this is what makes the argument below apply and should not
be confused with the ring-valued combination challenges µ1 , . . . , µN ∈ Rq [LNP22, Figure 7], which amortize
N separate quadratic equations into one via a completely different (degree-counting) soundness argument
and are not examined here. In our component-wise instantiation, that amortization step needs no K-valued
analogue at all: once every relation has already been expanded into RF -equations on (a, b) (§§3.3.2–3.3.3),
amortizing many such equations into one is done exactly as in the unmodified base protocol, entirely over
RF , with no reference to K.
    We now check that replacing the scalar γ ∈ Zq of the masking step with a scalar γ ∈ K (i.e. γ = γa + γb Y ,
γa , γb ∈ F) delivers the intended 1/q 2 soundness, for the corresponding RK -valued relation now represented
component-wise. Write fK (s) = fa + fb Y ∈ RK for the committed evaluation (so fa , fb ∈ RF ), and g =
ga + gb Y ∈ RK for the masking element, sampled so that g̃ = 0 in K (or, equivalently, since {1, Y } is an
F-basis, so that g̃a = g̃b = 0 in F). This is exactly two independent copies of the base-protocol masking
distribution (each of ga , gb uniform outside its constant coefficient, zero at the constant coefficient); no new
sampling procedure is required beyond doubling the existing one.
    The masking identity h = γ · fK (s) + g, with γa , γb ∈ F ⊂ RF acting as scalars, holds as an identity of full
RF -polynomials:
                               ha = γa fa + νγb fb + ga ,     hb = γa fb + γb fa + gb .

Remark 3.17 (Extension degree e > 2). Nothing is special to the quadratic extension K = F( ν) (degree
e = 2), and more secure and non-interactive versions will require e = 4. For a general K = F[Y ]/(g) with g
irreducible of degree e, replace the rank-doubling’s ambient by the regular representation K → Mate (RF ) in
the basis {1, Y, . . . , Y e−1 }; every RK -element is then committed as the e-tuple of its coordinates, an RK -linear
relation becomes e RF -linear ones, and a quadratic relation e RF -quadratic Pe−1 ones,2 by the same expansion as
in §3.3.2–§3.3.3. The norm convention of §3.3.4 becomes ∥x∥2 :=                 i=0 ∥ai ∥ , proved as e independent
RF -automorphism statements. The masking argument of §3.3.5 goes through verbatim: for γ ∈ K the matrix
M is the regular representation of fe ∈ K, so det M = NK/F (fe) ̸= 0 whenever fe ̸= 0, giving soundness
1/q e = 1/|K| as before; irreducibility of g replaces the non-residuosity of ν.
   The cost is the message-slot occupancy. Each RK -element consumes e of        Pthe ℓ BDLOP slots rather than 2,
so at a fixed ℓ the packed commitment counts scale by e/2, and with them           Nc and the masked-openings row
that dominates. Arithmetic over K likewise costs O(elog2 3 ) base-field multiplications under Karatsuba rather
than O(3). We do not give a tuned e = 4 parameter set here; re-optimising ℓ upward is the obvious first
move, since ℓ enters the commitment size linearly but Nc , and hence the dominant opening cost, inversely.
3.3.6. Complexity. We conclude by recording the cost of the component-wise instantiation.
   On the size axis, a linear RK -relation expands into two RF -relations on the stacked vector (a; b) of twice
the length, so the number of proved rows, the committed vector length, and hence the transmitted opening
all exactly double. On the arithmetic axis it does not double. By §3.3.2 the relation is applied through
                                                               
                                                       Aa νAb
(3.12)                                      ψ(AK ) =              ,
                                                       Ab Aa
a 2m×2k block matrix, so a matrix-vector product costs four RF products of the original shape, or three under
Karatsuba; the block structure is precisely what the Karatsuba method exploits. A quadratic relation carries
the same factor for the same reason: each RK -product (a1 + b1 Y )(a2 + b2 Y ) requires the four RF -products
a1 a2 , b1 b2 , a1 b2 , a2 b1 , three under Karatsuba at the cost of one extra addition. So both sub-protocol families
carry a factor 3 to 4 in prover and verifier work, and a factor 2 in communication.

3.4. Libra-style masking. Let f (X1 , . . . , Xℓ ) be aPmultivariate polynomial with partial degrees d⃗ =
(d1 , . . . , dℓ ) and assume that we wish to prove that ⃗x∈{0,1}ℓ f (⃗x) = H, for H a purported field value.
The Sum-Check protocol is an interactive protocol which proves the above equality. Unfortunately, the
protocol is inherently not zero-knowledge, as it discloses evaluations of f and f is constructed from an
instance-witness pair.
   To achieve a zero-knowledge Sum-Check, we must ensure that the execution transcript does not leak
information about the witness or intermediate circuit wires. The masking process is divided into two distinct
logical steps: hiding the circuit transcript on the hypercube, and hiding the intermediate round evaluations.
We recall only the required material here and refer the reader to [Tha22, §13.3] and [XZZ+ 19] for all the
details.
   We begin by recalling how to mask the intermediate rounds, following the masked Sum-Check protocol
devised in [XZZ+ 19, Construction 1, §4.1]. The protocol picks a sum of small random univariate polynomials
                           Pℓ                          Pdi
g(X1 , . . . , Xℓ ) = a0 + i=1 gi (Xi ) with gi (Xi ) = j=1 ai,j xji ; note that here we only require that the degrees
of the gi ’s correspond to the partial degrees of f .

Protocol 5 zkSumCheck: Zero-Knowledge Sum-Check Protocol
                                                          P
   Input: Polynomial f (x1 , . . . , xℓ ), claimed sum H = x∈{0,1}ℓ f (x), and individual degrees (d1 , . . . , dℓ ).
   Output: Accept or Reject the validity of H.
                                                LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                   31




    (acc/rej) ← zkSumCheck(f, H, ℓ, (d1 , . . . , dℓ )).
                                             Step 1: Prover Commitments
                                                                        Pℓ
 1: P selects a random masking polynomial g(x1 , . . . , xℓ ) = a0 +     i=1 gi (xi ).
                                 Pdi        j
    For each i ∈ [ℓ], gi (xi ) = j=1 ai,j xi where ai,j are uniformly random.
                       P
 2: P computes G =         x∈{0,1}ℓ g(x) and comg = Commit(g).
 3: P → V: Sends G and comg .
                                              Step 2: Verifier Challenge
 4: V uniformly selects challenge ρ ∈ F∗ .
 5: V → P: Sends ρ.
 6: Both parties compute the combined claim: H ′ = H + ρ · G.
                                            Step 3: Interactive Sum-Check  P
 7: P and V execute standard Sum-Check protocol on: H + ρ · G =              x∈{0,1}ℓ (f (x) + ρg(x)).
 8: At the final round, V obtains claim hℓ (rℓ ) for random point r = (r1 , . . . , rℓ ).
                                                   Step 4: Verification
 9: P ↔ V: At the final Sum-Check round, prover and verifier open the commitment of g at r: (g(r), π) ←
    Open(g, r).
10: V checks commitment: compute the Verify(comg , g(r), r, π). If reject, abort.
11: V computes the residue: vf = hℓ (rℓ ) − ρg(r).
12: return Compare vf with oracle access of f (r1 , . . . , rℓ ).



   Since g is a uniformly random polynomial with the same degree profile as f , and the verifier challenge ρ is
sampled after the prover commits to g, the polynomial f + ρg itself is not indistinguishable from a uniformly
random polynomial of the same degree profile; note that it still agrees with f on all but the single masking
term. What is masked, instead, are the Sum-Check round messages. Each per-round univariate polynomial
sent by the prover is statistically close to uniform (over the randomness of g), so the transcript leaks (up to
the caveat in Remark 3.18 below) no information about f or the witness beyond the final evaluation. The
simulator can generate an indistinguishable transcript of round messages by sampling g and ρ as above and
running the standard Sum-Check machinery on f + ρg, without access to the real witness f .
Remark 3.18.
  (1) As it stands, the Libra masking protocol is not completely zero-knowledge (cf. [Tha22, 13.3] or [CFW26])
      as it reveals the evaluation of f at ⃗r (from the opening of g at ⃗r). If we aim for total zero-knowledge,
      we must hide this value.
  (2) As long as the commitment scheme has opening and verification which is zero-knowledge, then the
      protocol is zero-knowledge (up to the final evaluation).
  (3) We will make use of the simulator constructed in [XZZ+ 19, Theorem 3] as a reference point, but
      not as our simulator. In our instantiation the mask is never opened, and the masking turns out
      to be perfect rather than statistical. The transcript, the final value h included, is uniform on its
      consistency class independently of the polynomial being summed (§4.1.1.1). We therefore simulate
      by sampling that class directly, at zero advantage.
3.5. Replacing the PCS with the ABDLOP commitment scheme. For instantiating the Libra-style
masking, we can either consider a multivariate polynomial commitment scheme over extension fields K, or,
                                                                                (1)
more conveniently, make use of the ABDLOP proof of knowledge (cf. Protocol Πmany ((s2 , s1 , m), (f1 , f2 , . . . , fN ))).
The natural candidates for a PCS approach would be a modification of the multilinear Hachi [NOZ26], whose
generic reduction sends evaluation claims over Fqe to claims over RF , or a split PCS approach built on the
univariate lattice-based scheme Greyhound [NS24]. The split-PCS route might seem attractive, but Grey-
hound’s whole value proposition is compressing a large polynomial, whereas the masking polynomial p is
small compared to the polynomials that genuinely need compression with a PCS: succinctness is irrelevant
when the object being committed to is already logarithmic in the size of what we are proving about, and
here we would pay ≈ d2 log m log q to compress something smaller than a single Sum-Check transcript. The
same objection applies to Hachi: it is a succinct scheme for large multilinear polynomials, and our p has
a few coefficients. We therefore adopt the ABDLOP-based route throughout, and record both alternatives
32                                                 LATTICEBLINDFOLD


only as points of comparison; it may still be of independent interest for larger masking polynomials in other
settings. For completeness, such a split-PCS scheme can be built by combining Greyhound, which satisfies an
honest-verifier zero-knowledge evaluation property, with a ring-switching embedding in the style of [FMN23,
§5.6], realizing K as (a component of) a cyclotomic ring over which Greyhound is defined; the trick is usu-
ally performed with primes q ≡ 5 (mod 8), and extends, following [BCS23, Lemma 7.3] and the cyclotomic
factorization results recalled in [Was97], to primes outside that residue class.
   Concretely, recall from §3.4 that we only need to
    (1) bind p before ζ is sampled,
    (2) hide p throughout, or the Sum-Check masking fails, and
    (3) convince V that the value p(⃗r ′ ) entering the final check is the evaluation of the committed p.
Nothing here requires the commitment or proof to be sublinear in p. And p is tiny: p(X)   ⃗ = a0 + Pℓ p̃i (Xi )
                                                                                                      i=1
has 1 + Dmax · ℓ coefficients in K, which, at b = 2, u ≤ 2, m = 221 , is 1 + 4 × 21 = 85 elements (two
ring elements of RK at d = 64, and one at d = 128; cf. the packing count ⌈(1 + Dmax log2 (m))/d⌉ used in
Protocol 6).
   The ABDLOP commitment scheme can be used for the evaluation as well. More precisely, we pack the
coefficients of p into ⌈(1 + Dmax log2 (m))/d⌉ ring elements and commit with CommitABDLOP , which takes
unbounded-norm messages. Then
(3.13)                                p(⃗r ′ ) = coeffs, (1, ri′ , ri′2 , ri′3 , ri′4 )i∈[ℓ]
is a public linear function of the committed message. This is exactly the relation u = R1 s1 + Rm m that
            (1)

committed coefficient vector, recoverable from the same commitment with R1 = 0 and the appropriate Rm .
   Honest-verifier zero-knowledge comes from SABDLOP (cf. Lemma 3.6), so Remark 4.1.(2)-type require-
ments are satisfied by the same simulator. We will see that, in practice, we do not reveal the evaluation p(r),
but use it inside a more general ABDLOP ZKPoK.
3.6. A brief recall on rejection sampling. Besides the changes regarding the commitments, we must
modify our protocols by including a rejection sampling algorithm, in the spirit of [LNP22, Lemma 2.14].
This is of utmost importance in order to achieve a blinding folding scheme.
   With the notation introduced in §§2.1–2.3, let us fix the rejection sampling parameter:
                                        √
      • s := ξ · (K + k) · T · (b − 1) · nF be a fixed standard deviation, and
      • ξ > 0.
We denote by Dsℓ the discrete Gaussian distribution on RℓF centered around 0 with standard deviation s
(see [LNP22, Definition 2.1]). Since we are working with infinity norms, we replace [LNP22, Lemma 2.2] (cf.
also [Ban93, Lemma 1.5.(i)]) by the following statement.
                                                                                                              2
Lemma 3.19 ([Ban93, Mic16]). Let z ← Dsℓ for (z ∈ RℓF ). Then Pr[∥z∥ > τλ,ξ · s] < 2 · ℓd · e−π·(τλ,ξ ) .
                                                                                               2
Note that in our setting nF ≈ ℓ · d. Lemma 3.19 gives Pr[∥z∥ > τλ,ξ s] < 2nF e−πτλ,ξ , so a tail probability of
at most 2−λ requires                        q                 
                                     τλ,ξ ≥    λ + log2 (2nF ) ln 2 / π .
At our working level λ = 120 this gives τλ,ξ ≈ 5.50 for nF = 216 and τλ,ξ ≈ 5.58 for nF = 220 ; at λ = 128 the
corresponding values are 5.657 and 5.734. We note that k depends on λ only through this square root. The
change from λ = 128 to λ = 120 alters τλ,ξ by under 3%.
Lemma 3.20 ([LNP22, Lemma 2.4, Figure 1]). Let V ⊆ RℓF be a set of polynomials with norm at most
    √                                                                                                   √
L · nF and ρ : V → [0, 1] be a probability distribution. Fix the standard deviation s = ξ · L · nF . Let
M = exp(14/ξ + 1/(2ξ 2 )) (the constant 14 is the one derived in [LNP22, Lemma 2.14] at λ = 128; we use it
unchanged at λ = 120, which is conservative). Now, sample v ← ρ and y ← Dsℓ , set z = y+v. There exists a
rejection sampling function Rej1 , with Rej1 (z, v, s) = 1 signaling a restart (following the standard convention
of [LNP22], so that Rej1 = 0 is the accepting outcome) such that the probability that Rej1 (z, v, s) accepts lies
                                               LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                  33


in (1 − 2−λ )/M, 1/M for every v ∈ V and the distribution of (v, z), conditioned on the acceptance of
                          

Rej1 (z, v, s), is within statistical distance 2−λ of the product distribution ρ × Dsℓ .
Note that the upper bound 1/M is immediate from the acceptance rule min{1, Dsℓ (z)/(M Ds,v
                                                                                         ℓ
                                                                                           (z))}, averaging
            ℓ
over z ← Ds,v  . Moreover, [LNP22] considers a security parameter of λ = 128 and thus, for ξ ≈ 13, we have
that the rejection repetition ends in ≈ 3 repetitions and for ξ ≈ 20 in ≈ 2 repetitions.
   In order to ensure the blinding property, we must combine the above parameters so as not to exceed the
                                                                 √
norm bound B = bk , that is, τλ,ξ · s < B (equivalently τλ,ξ ξ L nF < bk with L := (K + k) T (b − 1) as in
Lemma 3.20). With the above notation, we must have, for K = 1 = b − 1 (which is our use case, cf. §4.2-),
      • k = 26 for T = 128, d = 64, nF = 216 , and ξ ≈ 13,
      • k = 27 for T = 128, d = 64, nF = 216 , and ξ ≈ 20,
      • k = 29 for T = 128, d = 64, nF = 220 , and ξ ≈ 13,
      • k = 29 for T = 128, d = 64, nF = 220 , and ξ ≈ 20,
      • k = 27 for T = 256, d = 128, nF = 216 , and ξ ≈ 13 (this case is sensitive to λ: the bound moves to
        k = 28 for λ ≥ 127, and the margin at λ = 120 is under 0.05 bits),
      • k = 28 for T = 256, d = 128, nF = 216 , and ξ ≈ 20,
      • k = 30 for T = 256, d = 128, nF = 220 , and ξ ≈ 13,
      • k = 30 for T = 256, d = 128, nF = 220 , and ξ ≈ 20.
  Considering Definition 3.1, we also compute:
      • k = 29 for T = 128, d = 64, nF = 221 , and ξ ≈ 13,
      • k = 30 for T = 128, d = 64, nF = 221 , and ξ ≈ 20,
      • k = 30 for T = 256, d = 128, nF = 221 , and ξ ≈ 13,
      • k = 31 for T = 256, d = 128, nF = 221 , and ξ ≈ 20.

3.7. The relations. In order to obtain zero-knowledge we must consider a few new type of relations, namely,

the restricted output relation, the norm-bounded uncommitted CCS relation, and the unpadded norm-
bounded CCS. We highlight in pink the differences between these relation and the one recalled in §2.4.1.
   Recall that we denote by B̃ ≤ B (actually B̃ = b) be the fixed bound norm for the randomness of the
ABDLOP commitment scheme Commit.

defined by applying the commitment L.
   Unfortunately, this is still not enough for suitably defining a NovaBlindFold analogue.
Remark 3.27. Recall Definition 2.11. It hands the instances (u1 , u2 ) to S as input, so it asserts nothing
about them, and it says that the interaction leaks nothing beyond the public instances. Whether that is
enough for zero-knowledge therefore depends entirely on which relation one takes R′ to be.
   Read against R′ = CCS(b, L), it is not enough, and for two reasons at once. First, u2 = (c1 , x1 ) with
c1 = L(z1 ), and L is a compact Ajtai commitment carrying no salt, so c1 is a deterministic function of the
witness; handing it to S is handing over the very object the blinded layout exists to hide. Second, A would
output w2 in full, blinding block included, so an A setting e := 0 fixes c1 outright and Lemma 3.3 has no
hypothesis left to work with.
   Both defects disappear when we cut the input relation correctly. We will see that neither c1 nor e is in
fact an input to ΠLBF : the protocol takes its input uncommitted (Definition 3.26) and forms c1 itself, and
Definition 3.1.(1) has the honest prover sample e. Treating either as part of u2 describes a protocol other
than the one we run. Taking R′ to be the unpadded relation of Definition 3.28 (see below) puts both objects
where they belong, that is, e in the prover’s randomness and c1 in the transcript. Definition 2.11 may then
be applied verbatim.
Definition 3.28 (Unpadded norm-bounded CCS). Let s be an R1CS structure in the blinded layout of
Definition 3.1, with blinding coordinates Ibl of ring-length nR,bl , and write s◦ for the structure obtained by
deleting the rows and columns indexed by Ibl , so that s = pad(s◦ ) for the deterministic, public padding map of
that definition. We write CCS◦ (b)K for the relation CCS(b)K of Definition 3.26 taken over s◦ : its instances
are the public inputs x, and its witnesses the circuit part wcirc of the witness alone, with no blinding block.
The two relations are related by a randomised prover-side map
            ιbl : CCS◦ (b)K −→ CCS(b)K ⊂ CCS(b, L)K ,                   (s◦ ; x; wcirc ) 7−→   pad(s◦ ); x; [wcirc , e] ,
                                                                                                                       
(3.19)
              n
with e ← χb R,bl the block sampled by the honest prover, χb uniform on {c ∈ RF : ∥c∥∞ ≤ b − 1} as
in Lemma 3.3, and the second inclusion the deterministic one of Equation (3.18), given by applying L.
Everything we need of ιbl follows from one structural observation.
Lemma 3.29. Let s = pad(s◦ ) follow the blinded layout of Definition 3.1, and fix a public input x. Write
z◦ := [x, wcirc ] and z := [x, wcirc , e]. Then
                   (s; x; [wcirc , e]) ∈ CCS(b)    ⇐⇒     (s◦ ; x; wcirc ) ∈ CCS◦ (b) and ∥e∥∞ < b.
Equivalently, the fibre of CCS(b) over a given (x; wcirc ) is exactly {e : ∥e∥∞ < b}, the same set for every
(x; wcirc ).
Proof. The norm condition splits coordinatewise, ∥z∥∞ < b iff ∥z◦ ∥∞ < b and ∥e∥∞ < b. For the predicate,
f (M 1 z, . . . , Mt z) ∈ ZSlog2 (m) asks that the row values f ((M1 z)i , . . . , (Mt z)i ) vanish for every i, so we may
   g              g
treat the rows separately. For i ∈ Ibl , clause (3) of Definition 3.1 makes row i read zi · 1 − zi = 0, which
36                                                   LATTICEBLINDFOLD


holds whatever e is (Remark 3.2). For i ∈ / Ibl , clause (4) gives (Mj )i,Ibl = 0, whence (Mj z)i = (Mj◦ z◦ )i for
every j, so row i evaluates identically under s and under s◦ . The circuit rows therefore hold for z iff they
hold for z◦ , and the blinding rows hold always.                                                                ■
We have three consequences, in the order we use them.
    • ιbl is total : it is defined on the whole of CCS◦ (b)K and succeeds on every draw, never returning ⊥.
       What gives this is not merely that the fibre is non-empty but that it coincides with the support of
       the sampler. Lemma 3.29 identifies the fibre with {e : ∥e∥∞ < b}, which is exactly the support of
        n
       χb R,bl , so every value the prover can draw is a valid completion.
    • Truncation is well defined : Deleting the block is a map π : CCS(b) → CCS◦ (b) with π ◦ ιbl = id,
       so composing any extractor for Protocol 12)with π extracts for the final composition ΠLBF , at the
       same advantage and with no rewinding beyond what the former already does. Completeness and
       knowledge soundness transfer along ιbl unchanged; cf. Propositions 4.15 and 4.17.
    • The fibre does not depend on the circuit witness: e is drawn from a set fixed in advance by b and
       nR,bl alone, so its distribution is independent of anything an adversary A ,may choose.

                                                  4. LatticeBlindFold
   We are now ready to assemble our LatticeBlindFold protocol ΠLBF . We rework each of SuperNeo’s three
interactive reductions (ΠCCS , ΠRLC , and ΠDEC ) into its zero-knowledge counterpart, applying the tools recalled
and devised in §3.
4.1. The building blocks of LatticeBlindFold. We highlight in purple the changes we introduce re-
garding the masking technique, in pink the change of relation, in blue the modifications related to the
commitments, and in orange the ones related to the choice of independent challenges.
Remark 4.1.
  (1) We focus on the case of R1CS structures, that is, f has degree deg f = u ≤ 2; however the whole
      procedure can be generalized to handle CCS structures. The drawback in handling general structures
      is that the amount of commitments grows accordingly, quickly rendering the scheme impractical.
  (2) For brevity we denote by Commit the commitment CommitABDLOP in all the following sections, and
      by B̃ the coefficient bound from which commitment salts are sampled. We take B̃ = 2 (ternary salts)
      throughout, purely for concreteness . The relevant salt bounds are Bfold of Lemma B.1, on the folded
      salt, and βrlx of Definition 3.22, on extracted openings, both independent of k.
  (3) We assume the number of constraints to be equal to the size of the instance-witness m = nF a power
      of two with d | nF , so that the witness is a ring vector of length nR = nF /d and Mj ∈ Fm×m . We
      write the R1CS predicate as f (Y1 , Y2 , Y3 ) = Y2 · Y3 − Y1 and take M1 = Im , which is without loss of
      generality for constraint systems arising from circuits (as every multiplication gate owns its output
      wire). We assume throughout that s and the witnesses follow the blinded layout of Definition 3.1.
      That is, the last nF,bl rows are pass-through gates imposing no constraint, while M1 = Im and the
      coverage of NC are preserved (Remark 3.2).
4.1.1. First Reduction: R1CS Reduction.

Before proceeding further we discuss the following remarks.
                                                    LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                       39


Remark 4.2.
    (1) Π′R1CS does not by itself certify the hint commitments. Soundness of the {Commit(yi,j             ′
                                                                                                            , s′i,j )} is dis-
                                              ′                                                             ′
        charged by membership of w2 in R2 , not by Protocol 6. This is soundness relative to R2 in the sense
        of [NS26], together with the two-run witness-agreement condition, and is exactly what Lemma 4.9
        establishes; it should not be read as a failure of soundness.
    (2) As noticed above, we use a commitment scheme, like BDLOP [BDL+ 16] [LNP22, §1.3] or ABD-
        LOP [LNP22], which is not succinct; it is important to stress that a succinct commitment scheme,
        like Ajtai’s one, cannot be employed in our protocol since we need a compatibility between the com-
        mitments of ring elements and the commitments of their associated coefficients.
                                                                ′                 ′
    (3) One might consider passing commitments to f (ct(yi,1       ), . . . , ct(yi,t )) directly in the above Protocol 6
        however, this is likely not feasible. This is because the commitments we consider are lattice-based, and
        both the salts and the witnesses would end up with norms that are too large. Note that by considering
        R1CS structures and a small base b allows us to handle a reasonable amount of commitments, which
        would be exponential in the case of a general CCS structure.
    (4) The randomness δ0 and δ1 is sampled independently of γ, rather than being taken as further powers
        γ K , γ 2K+k of the single batching challenge as in [NS26, Appendix B.2]. The reason is a soundness
        one, and it is the same reason the three ⃗γ blocks are independent rather than powers of one another:
        item (2) of Lemma 4.16 needs the monomials of the three families to be distinguishable, and item (1)
        needs each block to enter with degree 1; cf. the proof of Lemma 4.9 and Proposition 4.17.
    (5) The attentive reader would have recalled that we are still committing to the instance-witness couple
        via L (recall Remark 2.23), thus, why is it necessary to consider different commitments? This is due
        to two (conflicting) reasons:
           • we necessitate of a non-succinct commitment scheme CommitABDLOP (as noticed above), and
           • we wish to minimize the transmission and proof costs, therefore, any commitments which can
               be taken to be succinct must be so.
    (6) The anchoring salt in §3.2.1.1 ensures that we are binding T to the initial witness and that the final
        evaluations implies that Sum-Check has been correctly handled.
    (7) Passing σ = ζ P + T in plain discloses nothing about T. Indeed P = x∈{0,1}log2 m p(x) = 2log2 m a0 +
                                                                                        P

        (·) is uniform in K, since a0 is uniform and 2log2 m ̸≡ 0 (mod q) is invertible; as ζ ∈ K× , the product
        ζ P is uniform as well, so σ is uniform regardless of T and T is perfectly hidden.
    (8) By considering the ABDLOP commitment scheme instead of a PCS, we replace the final evaluation
        check with a proof of knowledge, so that neither the masking polynomial’s evaluation nor the real one
        is ever revealed. Explicitly, matching the notation of Protocol 5, the check certified is
                                                    ′
                                        hlog2 (m) (rlog 2 (m)
                                                              ) − ζ · p(⃗r ′ ) = Q(⃗r ′ ),
                                  ′
         where h := hlog2 (m) (rlog       ) is the prover’s last-round Sum-Check message evaluated at the random
                                    2 (m)
         point (public, exactly as in ordinary Sum-Check) and p(⃗r ′ ) is the masking polynomial’s value at ⃗r ′
         (hidden, since p itself must stay hidden). This is precisely where we depart from [XZZ+ 19]: there the
         verifier obtains g(⃗r) from the opening of the polynomial commitment and recovers the real evaluation
         as vf = h−ρ g(⃗r), which is the residual leak recorded in Remark 3.18.(1). Here p(⃗r ′ ) is never opened,
         so Q(⃗r ′ ) is never formed as a field element in V’s view. The target u⋆ := h−eq(r′ , α)(cF +δ0 cN )−δ1 cE
         is computed by V from quantities already in its view rather than transmitted, and, because cF , cN , cE
         carry their salt aggregates (Step 16), it equals ζ p(⃗r ′ ) offset by the unopened salt aggregate of the
         Step 9–15 commitments. That offset is exactly what the weights {wn } of Step 18 account for, and it
         is what lets the final check be phrased as a single constant-coefficient linear relation in the committed
         messages, with a public right-hand side that V assembles from the transmitted BDLOP components
         (Lemma 3.10). The remaining public scalar of the masked rounds is h itself, which is harmless: see
         §4.1.1.1.
4.1.1.1. What V actually sees. Only three kinds of scalar reach V in the masked rounds. Transmitted : the
Sum-Check round polynomials and hence the final value h = (ζp + Q)(⃗r ′ ), the claimed sum σ = ζP + T, the
commitments, and the challenges. Derived by V, never transmitted: cF , cN , cE (Step 16) and u⋆ (Step 17),
which are deterministic functions of the former and so leak nothing further. Never revealed : the mask p
and its evaluation p(⃗r ′ ); the evaluations Q(⃗r ′ ), F(⃗r ′ ), NC(⃗r ′ ), Eval(⃗r ′ ); every hint yi,j
                                                                                                     ′
                                                                                                         and surrogate ŷi,j
40                                                 LATTICEBLINDFOLD


with its square, cube and product; and every salt. These are hidden by MLWE under freshly sampled salts,
and are what SABDLOP and the simulator of Lemma 3.16 replace in hybrid H1 of Theorem 4.13 (including
the invocations of Steps 1 and 17 of Protocol 7); for σ, see Remark 4.2.(7). Outside the masked rounds, V
additionally holds u1 itself. x1 is public by definition of the relation; c1 is a deterministic function of z1 , and
over the committed relation it would be part of the instance and so handed to S outright. Over the unpadded
relation of Definition 3.28 it is instead a prover message, and the blinded layout makes it statistically close
to uniform (Lemma 3.3), which is what Theorem 4.13 exploits. The layout alone does not suffice here; see
Remark 3.27. Without that layout the blinding guarantee is relative to c1 , and the end-to-end claim would
rest on the one-wayness rather than the hiding of L (however, without achieving zero-knowledge).
   That leaves the transcript itself, where the mask gives    P more than a statistical-distance
                                                                                P                   estimate would.
Write ℓ = log2 (m) and di = degXi Q. The mask p = a0 + i p̃i (Xi ) has 1+ i di uniform coefficients, and the
consistent transcripts for degrees (di )i∈[ℓ] form an affine space of the same dimension, each hi contributing
                                                            ′
di + 1 coefficients constrained by hi (0) + hi (1) = hi−1 (ri−1 ). For fixed Q and ζ ∈ K× the map between them
is triangular, the coefficients of p̃i acting on the non-constant coefficients of hi by 2ℓ−i in round i, and a0
on the claimed sum by 2ℓ , hence bijective for odd q. So for uniform p the whole transcript, h included, is
uniform independently of Q: the masking is perfect, and it is unaffected by γ, δ0 , δ1 , ⃗r ′ being public, since
                                                                                                            P the
randomness doing the work is p, which V never sees. The same computation gives P = 2ℓ a0 + 2ℓ−1 i,j ai,j
uniform, whence the uniformity of ζP + T.
4.1.1.2. Soundness cost of the batched degree-0 check. The degree-0 surrogates {ŷi,j }i∈[K+k],j∈[t] of Protocol 6
                                        (1)
(Step 10) are certified via a single Πmany call for the vector of N = 2(K +k)t linear relations, one row per (i, j)
                                                                     (1)
and per relation type. This vector-batching itself is free, as Πmany natively proves u = R1 s1 + Rm m ∈ RN                K
                                                                                    (1)
for an arbitrary N , with no extra challenge and no soundness cost beyond Πmany ’s own (cf. Lemma 3.6).
   What is not free is collapsing, within each single ŷi,j , the d − 1 scalar conditions cf(ŷi,j )ℓ = 0, ℓ = 2, . . . , d,
                                      (1)                                                                 Pd
into the one RK -valued row that Πmany requires. We do this via the packaged rotation πµ = ℓ=2 µℓ τℓ of
                                               $
Lemma 3.13, sampling a single {µℓ }dℓ=2 ← Kd−1 shared across all (K + k)t surrogates. This is what lets N
stay at 2(K + k)t rather than growing to d(K + k)t: without the collapse the first family would contribute
(d − 1)(K + k)t rows and the second, whose members are one condition each, a further (K + k)t.
    Each resulting row ct(πµ · ŷi,j ) = 0 is, however, only a constant-term statement, namely, πµ ŷi,j is a nonzero
                                              (1)
ring element for an honest prover, so Πmany cannot certify it directly (cf. §3.2.1). We route the whole family
                                                               (ct)
of N = 2(K + k)t constant-term conditions through Πmany (Protocol 4), which folds all of them into a single
                                                                                  (1)
full-ring row under its own challenge vector ⃗γ ∈ KN before invoking Πmany , rather than certifying each row
on its own.
    This is where the actual soundness cost of batching sits. Fix a cheating prover with some ŷi0 ,j0 not degree-
0. By Lemma 3.13 (applied to the single ring element ŷi0 ,j0 ), ct(πµ · ŷi0 ,j0 ) ̸= 0 except with probability at most
1/|K| over {µℓ }. Such bound is unaffected by how many surrogates are batched alongside it. Conditioned on
                                                                            (ct)
that row being nonzero, Lemma 3.8 bounds the probability that Πmany still accepts by 1/|K| over ⃗γ . Thus,
the extracted identity is affine in the single coordinate γj0 attached to the offending row, with every other
γj and the garbage term g held fixed, so this is a Schwartz–Zippel bound on one row rather than a union
bound over N of them. Now, a union bound over these two (mutually independent) failure events gives a
                                                                                        2
total knowledge-soundness cost for the batched degree-0 check of at most |K|               (cf. Step 12 of Protocol 6).

4.1.1.5. A remark on soundness of Protocol 6 as a whole. Anchoring σ and the verifier-computed u⋆ via Πanc
is what lets these checks be knowledge-sound rather than merely a formal identity a cheating prover could
satisfy with an arbitrary field value. Lemma 3.16 guarantees that whatever σ and u⋆ the verifier accepts
                                                                  (n)
are provably the asserted functions of the same (m(n) , s2 ) underlying the commitments opened earlier in
the protocol. This is exactly the extraction hook used in the proof of Lemma 4.9, and together with the
ordinary ZKPoK is what lets the extractor of Protocol 14 pull out consistent salts {si,j }.
4.1.2. Second Reduction: Random Linear Combination. Besides the commitment modifications, we
must guarantee that the distribution of the random linear combination can be simulated. To this goal, we
employ the results recalled in §3.6. Note that the algorithm still maintains its PPT structure. We highlight
such modification in brown.

   The mask y, and the quantities cy , xy , yy,j derived from it, are never scaled by any ρi : they are fixed
once, before ρ1 , . . . , ρK+k are drawn (and only ever resampled together, as a block, on a Rej1 rejection), and
enter c, x, yj additively. In particular y never occupies one of the K + k challenge-indexed slots.
   The mask y must precede the challenge. Lemma 3.20 is a statement about a specific sampling procedure:
y ← DsnF drawn independently of the secret v it is meant to hide, P           so that a simulator can reproduce the
marginal of z = v + y without ever knowing v. In Protocol 7, v := i∈[k+K] ρi zi depends on the verifier’s
own challenge ρK+1 , . . . , ρK+k , so it cannot itself play the role of the lemma’s independent mask. Conversely,
y is sampled by the prover before ρ1 , . . . , ρK+k are even drawn, and added unscaled by any ρi . This is what
lets us invoke Lemma 3.20 honestly, with v playing the role of the arbitrary secret and y the role of the
genuine Gaussian mask.
                                                                                l                  m
Remark 4.3 (On the Budget Attempt). Recall that (§2.1) Wmax := λ log2 M/(M − 1) is the attempt
budget, where M is the repetition rate of the rejection sampling function Rej1 . Wmax is public and known
to both parties, and V can enforce it because it samples ρ         ⃗ afresh on every iteration. Enforcement costs
one bit per attempt (P signals accept or restart, the two cases being distinguished by whether an opening
or a fresh commitment follows) but no additional round. An honest P exhausts the budget with probability
                                                     LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                           43


at most (1 − 1/M )Wmax ≤ 2−λ ; this forces our protocols to be statistically complete rather than achieving
perfect completeness. Let us remark that this bound is what makes the extraction argument of Lemma 4.10
go through; see Remark B.2. At the working level λ = 120 fixed in §2.1 this is Wmax = 206 for M ≈ 3
and Wmax = 120 for M ≈ 2 (for λ = 128, Wmax = 219, 128 respectively). Unlike M , which governs the
expected number of attempts, Wmax is a worst-case bound enforced by V and is the quantity that enters the
knowledge-soundness error; cf. Remark B.2. See Proposition 4.15.
4.1.2.1. Batching convention.
Remark 4.4 (Batching the Π′RLC proofs of knowledge). Step 1 of Protocol 7 is stated as (K + k)t separate
                                                                                                          (1)
invocations, one per commitment, purely for readability. They can be collapsed into a single call to Πmany
on the concatenation of the (K + k)t salt blocks, exactly as Step 5 of Protocol 8 already does on (k + 1)t
                                                                                         (1)
of them, and likewise the t invocations of Step 17. Nothing in the analysis changes: Πmany natively proves
                       N
u = R1 s1 + Rm m ∈ RK for arbitrary N , and the vector-batching is free both in soundness (§4.1.1.2) and,
now, in completeness, since one rejection loop replaces (K + k + 1)t of them.
   The gain is worth recording, because this term dominates the completeness error of the whole protocol.
Batched, εRLC
          c   drops from (1 + (K + k + 1)t)2−λ to 3 · 2−λ and the consolidated εLBF
                                                                                c   of Proposition 4.15 scales
accordingly.
    It is worth fixing, once and for all, the convention under which every completeness count in this paper is
stated, since it is the only source of asymmetry between the three reductions.
    Our protocols are written with the ABDLOP proofs of knowledge spelled out invocation by invocation, one
per commitment, because that is the form in which the extraction arguments of Lemmas 4.9–4.12 are easiest
to read: each invocation certifies exactly one salt block, and the extractor recovers exactly one opening from
it. Step 1 of Protocol 6 and Step 5 of Protocol 8 are displayed already batched, whereas Steps 1 and 17 of
Protocol 7 are not. This asymmetry is an accident of exposition and not one of substance. By Remark 4.4
                                     (1)
the latter two collapse into two Πmany calls at no cost in soundness, in communication, or in the extractor,
          (1)
since Πmany natively proves u = R1 s1 + Rm m ∈ RN     K for arbitrary N .
    Accordingly, the batched form is the intended implementation path, and every count of the shape NPoK =
6 + (K + k + 1)t, together with every completeness error derived from it, is reported as a conservative
upper bound that can be read directly off the displayed protocols. Under the default batched path the same
statements read
(4.5)                           NPoK = 8,          εRLC
                                                    c   ≤ 3 · 2−λ ,          εLBF
                                                                              c   ≤ 9 · 2−λ ,
uniformly in k and t, and deduplicating as in Remark 4.14 brings NPoK further down to 7.

commitment is certified by a batched proof of knowledge rather than by a homomorphic identity.

Remark 4.5 (Why the salts are resampled rather than decomposed). Decomposing the salt might be degen-
erate. Lemma B.1 bounds a folded salt by Bfold = (K + k)T (C)(B̃ − 1) + B̃, whereas a splitB̃ into k pieces
inherits k from the witness budget τλ,ξ s < bk of §3.6. The two budgets differ by exactly the rejection-sampling
blow-up                                                  √
                             τλ,ξ ξ (K + k) T (C) (b − 1) nF           √
                                                               = τλ,ξ ξ nF ≈ 217.3
                                  (K + k) T (C) (B̃ − 1)
           21
at nF = 2 , ξ ≈ 20, τλ,ξ ≈ 5.60 (the λ = 120 value of §3.6), so that ≈ 17 of the k ∈ {30, 31} digits are
identically zero. For those indices s1,i,j = s2,i,j = 0 and the ABDLOP commitment degenerates and the eval-
uation hint would be transmitted in the clear. Similarly, Lemma 3.6 would not apply to those commitments,
since SABDLOP samples (tA , tB ) uniformly and no zero-salt commitment resembles a uniform pair.

It is crucial that the blinding step is run at first, otherwise the final Sum-Check evaluation would break
zero-knowledge. We addressed the branch of Protocol 13 in which the optional input is ⊥ and the prover
samples the k accumulator instances itself via Protocol 11. In the other branch the accumulator is supplied
from outside, no fresh sampling occurs, and nothing above applies to it. We stress again that this is an
informal design principle, not a proof. Formalizing it requires a preservation statement, of the shape: if
                                              LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                 47


the input accumulator is distributed as sampleblind then so is the output. Such statement must be proved
inductively over rounds. We have not established it, and we flag two places where it is not free. First,
the mask of Protocol 7 is drawn afresh each round, so the per-round argument of Appendix B.4 does go
through round by round; what is missing is that the input to round r + 1 be in the restricted relation of
Definition 3.25, which is a hypothesis on round r’s output that only the induction supplies. Second, the
extraction side compounds independently, and badly, for the reason given in Remark 4.8.
Remark 4.7 (The folded structure must satisfy clause (4)). Clause (4) of Definition 3.1, namely, that the
circuit rows carry no support on the blinding columns, is a hypothesis on the structure s handed to ΠLBF−Fold ,
and it is worth checking rather than assuming. Whenever the layout is reached by appending nF,bl fresh
variables together with their nF,bl fresh rows to an existing s◦ it holds automatically, the pre-existing rows
mentioning none of the new columns, and this is how every structure in this paper arises. The case to
confirm is the recursive one. On iterating ΠLBF−Fold , the structure folded at round r + 1 encodes the verifier
of round r, and that circuit is built first and padded afterwards, so clause (4) holds there by construction as
well. It now does so for a stated reason rather than an implicit one, which is the point of recording it: an
implementation that interleaved the blinding variables into the circuit’s own variable numbering, instead of
appending them, would meet clauses (1)–(3) and fail clause (4) silently.
   What would break is not a technicality. Without clause (4) the fibre of Lemma 3.29 depends on the circuit
witness, so ιbl may fail on some inputs and truncation is no longer well defined. The transfer of knowledge
soundness along ιbl goes with it, along the need the distribution of e to be fixed in advance by b and nR,bl
alone.
Remark 4.8 (Single invocation versus iteration). Step 5 of Protocol 8 certifies the salts of its input com-
mitment only in the relaxed sense of Definition 3.22. By Lemma 3.6(2) the extractor obtains s̄j with
∥c̄ s̄j ∥2 ≤ βrlx , not ∥s̄j ∥∞ < Bfold . For a single invocation of ΠLBF (the main use case this work is pri-
marily aimed at, where folding is used once to introduce zero-knowledge on top of a given R1CS circuit)
this is exactly what is needed, for three reasons. The output witness of Protocol 8 is the honest prover’s, so
its norms are a hypothesis of the reduction-of-knowledge definition (membership of (u2 , w2 ) in R2 ) rather
than a claim to be certified. The extracted R1CS witness z1 , which is the object one actually cares about, is
bounded exactly and within the same round by the norm-check polynomial NC(X)        ⃗ of Protocol 6, Step 3, with
no salt involved. And the remaining relaxed objects are the prover’s own sampled blinding instances of Step 1
of Protocol 12. Indeed the final deliverable of ΠLBF is a witness for R′ = CCS(b, L) (Definition 2.21), which
carries no salt component at all. More precisely, every salt clause in the intermediate relations is bookkeeping
that keeps the composition type-correct, and its tightness never surfaces in the statement of Theorem 4.13.
Relaxation therefore costs only the MSIS bound of Remark 3.23, and nothing at all in blinding.
    Iterating ΠLBF−Fold is a different matter. There the witness at round r is not supplied by an honest prover
but produced by the extractor of round r + 1, so its salt bound must be certified rather than assumed, and
R2 -membership discharges the norm clauses only at the last round. The relaxation then compounds: each
round contributes a factor c̄ from Lemma 3.6(2) and a factor (ρi − ρ′i ) from the coordinate-wise inversion of
Lemma 2.19. The number of rounds supported is therefore O(1), and small.
    To fold without such a bound, the fresh salts must be certified exactly. The device is the exact ℓ2 -norm
proof of [LNP22, §5.2], whose translation to RK is the content of §3.3.4, and which reaches the BDLOP
randomness s2 (untouched by [LNP22]’s norm machinery, which targets√the committed        p part (s1 , m)) through
the anchoring instantiation of   p§3.2.1.1.  One would  append  it, at bound kt (B̃ − 1)  (m1 + m2 )d, to Step 5
above and, at bound (B̃ − 1) t(m1 + m2 )d, to the proof of knowledge at Step 17 of Protocol 7, whose mask
salts are subject to the same objection. The extracted salts would then satisfy their bounds with no factor c̄,
binding of Commit would identify the certified opening at round r with the one the extractor of round r + 1
recovers, and nothing would accumulate. This is the salt-side analogue of the role NC(X)    ⃗ already plays for
the witness in [NS26], namely, an exact norm certificate, introduced precisely so that extraction need not
divide by challenge differences.

complete, sound, and zero-knowledge protocol) to Appendix B.

Proof. Equations (4.7)–(4.9) are the completeness clauses of the three lemmas cited, each a union bound
over the failure events restricted to the reduction in question, plus, for Π′RLC , the mask loop. The mask-loop
bound is Remark 4.3. Equation (4.10) then follows from the composition clause of Proposition 2.8, applied

   Step 1 of Protocol 6 is a single Πmany call on the concatenation of the kt salt blocks and so carries one
rejection loop, whence the 5 of Equation (4.7). Steps 1 and 17 of Protocol 7 are written as (K + k)t and
t separate calls and so carry that many, hence Equation (4.8). The asymmetry is one of exposition, not of
substance; the batched figures and the convention under which all of these counts are reported are fixed in
§4.1.2.1.
   Two independence claims are used and are worth isolating, since they are what makes the union bound
legitimate rather than merely convenient. First, distinct invocations use independently sampled masks
(y1 , y2 ), so their abort events are independent. This holds even for invocations that share a commitment,
since we freeze the commitment and resamples only the mask. Second, the ABDLOP retry budgets are
nested strictly inside the mask loop of Protocol 7 and cannot interact with it. A proof-of-knowledge retry
never resamples the folding challenges ρ    ⃗, and a mask-loop iteration never resamples a proof-of-knowledge
challenge c.                                                                                                  ■

would lead to a suitable parameter choice. We stress that the terms below are knowledge-soundness terms
only: the MLWE hiding advantages are blinding terms and are accounted for separately, in Theorem 4.13.

4.3.3. Efficiency measures. We provide here a brief account of the complexity of the LatticeBlindFold
protocol and we compare it to SuperNeo and Nova, the most closely related folding schemes. Considering
only the complexity in terms of constraint sizes, that is, number of constraints m = nF , decomposition
number k = Θ(log2 m), and sizes nF and nF,in , we obtain the following Table 1.
   Let us recall that the value k ≈ log2 (m) used for both SuperNeo and LatticeBlindFold below is not a free
choice. It is the decomposition number that the rejection sampling underlying ΠLBF ’s blinding forces upon
it (Remark 4.18). We report ΠSuperNeo at this same k purely so that the two columns describe the same
protocol structure and can be compared metric by metric. However, ΠSuperNeo carries no such obligation. It
only needs k = Θ(1), at which its Prover Time, Verifier Time, and Communication all improve by a further
Θ(log2 (m)) factor over what the table shows. That gap is, in essence, the structural cost of adding blinding,
on top of the additive nF,in term discussed below. A second, less visible cost sits inside the constants rather
than in the table: ΠLBF transmits the public part of the k blinding pairs its prover samples (k compact Ajtai
commitments and kt evaluation hints), which ΠSuperNeo has no analogue of. Being Θ(k) = Θ(log2 (m)), this
is absorbed by the log2 (m) term already present in the Communication row and by the kκd contribution, so
the asymptotics below stand as written.The blinding block of Definition 3.1 enters every count only through
nF , which it increases by nF,bl = Θ(κd log q).

and an RF -multiplication is Θ(d log d) of the latter. The size parameters differ too as Nova is linear in the
52                                            LATTICEBLINDFOLD


number of nonzero entries of the constraint matrices (here approximated to Ω(m)), whereas the Sum-Check

the blinding block of Definition 3.1. The two agree up to a constant for circuit-derived R1CS.

We obtain the values k = 29 (at nF = 220 ) and k = 30, 31 (at the nF = 221 ) tabulated in §3.6. On the other

   Table 1 reports both lattice schemes with k = Θ(log nF ) that Equation (4.17) imposes on us. Every log2 (nF )
factor appearing there is a k-factor, and it is charged to both schemes alike. Since ΠSuperNeo is not obliged
to run at this k, but at the smaller k = Θ(1) permitted by Equation (4.16) its costs improve to
                                                                                                   
         Prover O(d nF ),       Verifier O d + nF,in + log2 (m) ,    Communication O d + log2 (m) ,
i.e. a Θ(log nF ) improvement across the board. That gap is the price of blinding, and we wish to stress it
here.
   Unfortunately, the constants in the asymptotic complexity are bigger for the LatticeBlindFold protocol,
in particular, the proof size grows due to the masking techniques we employ (cf. §4.3.3.1). However, if
we are interested in introducing zero-knowledge on top of a protocol, we can wrap the initial protocol in
an efficient SNARK and only then apply our LatticeBlindFold technique, so that the nF these figures scale
in is the SNARK verifier’s circuit rather than the original computation’s. The sizes above are the cost of
producing the folded claims, not of checking them, and the two should not be confused. ΠLBF terminates
                                                                            (1)
with k pairs in CEcom (b, L, B̃, Commit, T, K) and with the transcripts of NPoK = 5 + (K + k + 1)t ABDLOP
proofs of knowledge; a verifier convinced by them is convinced of the original R1CS statement, but only
after checking them. Read as NovaBlindFold is read, this leaves a decider to be supplied, and there are
two ways to supply it. The direct one hands the openings to the downstream verifier unchanged and a
linear scan of them. The succinct one arithmetizes the ABDLOP verification equations tA = A1 s1 + A2 s2 ,
tB = Bs2 + m together with the masked-opening
                                                     norm checks, and proves them inside a further R1CS
instance; that circuit is Θ Nc (m1 + m2 )d ring operations and the norm checks are its awkward part, being
the same exact-ℓ2 machinery Remark 4.8 needs. We instantiate neither, and record the gap as such.

t = 3, nF = 221 , ξ ≈ 20, with k = 31 at d = 128 and k = 30 at d = 64), a single folding step costs ≈ 8.2 resp.
≈ 5.5 MB, distributed as follows.

                    total                                            ≈ 8.2 MB    ≈ 5.5 MB
The commitment counts behind the two columns are 88+31 ABDLOP and 32+31 compact Ajtai at d = 128,
against 85 + 30 and 31 + 30 at d = 64. The unit costs are 23.05 KB and 14.38 KB per ABDLOP commitment
                                             LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                53


respectively, and 15 KB resp. 13 KB per compact Ajtai commitment; the ABDLOP figure is κd(log2 q − D)
bits for the compressed tA together with ℓd log2 q for tB , the compact Ajtai one κAjtai-Compact d log2 q. The
gap between the columns is not the packing width, which is identical at the two degrees: both sets run at
ℓ = 16, hence at ℓ/2 = 8 RK -messages per commitment, and the counts above differ only through k. Nor does
the halved ring degree cancel, notwithstanding that a full-width ring element is 0.5 KB at d = 64 against
1 KB at d = 128. It cancels only where the dimension is MSIS/MLWE-driven and so roughly doubles as d
halves: the compact Ajtai commitment moves by 13% (κAjtai-Compact going 15 → 26) and the tA part of an
ABDLOP commitment by 9% (κ going 11 → 19). Wherever the dimension is instead fixed independently of
d it does not cancel at all: tB costs ℓd log2 q at ℓ = 16 and so halves outright, from 131 072 to 65 536 bits,
and the z1 half of every masked opening, at m1 = 15, halves with it. Since the object counts are set by the
instance index (K + k and k) rather than by ring capacity, they barely move (119 ABDLOP commitments
against 115) and the per-object saving is not bought back. Against that saving, the d = 64 set delivers
≈ 116 bits rather than ≈ 121 (Remark 4.19), so the two rows are not interchangeable. The two commitment
rows count both the objects the reduction outputs and the public part of the k blinding pairs the prover
samples at Step 1 of Protocol 12, which V must receive in order to fold; the latter account for ≈ 1.15 MB
of the total. Their public inputs xblind
                                       i    are not charged separately, as V derives all k of them from one
transmitted instance. One call in particular sets the widths. Of the eight batched ABDLOP invocations a
folding step makes, exactly one masks a folded salt: Step 5 of Protocol 8, whose input Cj is the output of
Π′RLC and carries ∥sl ∥∞ ≤ Bfold − 1 by Lemma B.1. Every other call (the sampler of Protocol 11, Steps 9–10
of Protocol 6, the mask salts, and the output of a previous Π′DEC , which resamples) sees ternary salts only,
and this is exactly what the resampling of Remark 4.5 buys. The widths must therefore be read per call:
the folded call runs at log2 s1 = 28.58 against ≈ 18.1 for the fresh ones at d = 128. It is the widest of the
eight, but neither the longest (Nc = 32, against the 53 of the Step 18 anchoring call) nor the largest single
contributor, accounting for 861 KB of the 4675 KB of masked openings against the 955 KB of Step 18. What
singles it out is the cost per block, 26.9 KB against 18.0 KB, and that is the price of the folded salt.
   We conclude by remarking that we achieve zero-knowledge and plausible post-quantum hardness at the
expense of bigger proofs and an overburdened prover (when compared to SuperNeo and Nova), but that
such trade-off is less steep when we keep in mind that Nova needs to work over a large 254-bit field, while
our LatticeBlindFold protocol works over small fields of ≈ 64 bits. Moreover, the lattice-framework is prone
to improvements based on parallelization, which lends itself to GPU-based speedups; possible improvements
are likely to be found in techniques devised by the Midnight Foundation in their implementation of Night-
stream [Fou25] (and SuperNeo [NS26]).

4.3.4. Choice of parameters. We record here two explicit instantiations, kept as close as possible to those
of [NS26, §8]. The Ajtai-side MSIS security is estimated with the SageMath [The25] routines of [NS26,
Appendix B.5–B.6] (cf. [APS15]), and the combined MLWE/MSIS security on the ABDLOP side with the
code of [LNP22, §6.1] (https://github.com/khanhcrypto/LBZKP), from which we drop the approximate
range proofs ([LNP22, Lemmas 2.7, 2.9]) and approximate
                           (1)         (2)                         −d/2

with CABDLOP and λABDLOP the strong sampling set and security parameter of the ABDLOP scheme.
Throughout we take the Solinas prime q = 264 − 59, a padded circuit of m = nF = 221 , ξ ≈ 20, K =
(b − 1) = (B̃ − 1) = 1, and C the set of polynomials with coefficients in {−1, 0, 1, 2}, so that |C| = 4d . The
decomposition number k is then forced by §3.6, and T , for the ABDLOP commitments, is the negacyclic
bound of Remark 2.17 rather than the weaker Proposition 2.16.
   One reading convention applies to both sets. A folding step makes eight batched ABDLOP invocations
on concatenated instances (Definition 3.15), whose masking widths are not a single pair: they depend on the

   In neither case is the lattice layer what binds. Both sets meet the δ < 1.0045 targeted in [LNP22, §6.1],
and at d = 128 the returned δMSIS = 1.004491 clears it only narrowly: the search returns the smallest
admissible dimension, and a practitioner should prefer κ = 12, at ≈ 0.6 KB per commitment, rather than
sit at the boundary. Since ℓ = 16 is even and K/F is quadratic, each of these commitments carries exactly
ℓ/2 = 8 elements of RK with no slot wasted. The parity remark applies to them alone: the blocks of Cy,0
carry native RF -vectors, so no packing arises there and all ℓ slots of a block are used (Remark 4.21).
Remark 4.19 (Consistency at λ). We fix λ = 120 as the working level, and every bit count above is an
interactive figure.
                                                LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                       55


    (1) The d = 64 set reaches ≈ 116 bits and so falls short of λ = 120; it is illustrative and should not
        be taken as a secure instantiation. What limits it is the challenge-space term Wmax (K + k)/|C| at
        |C| ≈ 2128 , not the lattice layer, so raising it calls for a larger C rather than larger commitment
        dimensions, at the price of a larger T (C) feeding back into Bfold and α. Comparing smaller base
        fields against higher-degree extensions would be interesting.
    (2) The d = 128 set reaches ≈ 121 bits under the independent-challenge default, the binding term being
        the Sum-Check error εSC = log2 (m)Dmax /|K|. Reaching λ = 128 would need |K| ≳ 2134 , hence a
        cubic extension or a larger base prime.
    (3) The lattice figures follow the methodology of [LNP22, §6.1], which measures hardness by the root
        Hermite factor; cf. item (3) of §5.
Remark 4.20 (Cost of the blinding block). The block adds nF,bl columns and, the system being square,
nF,bl rows. Two denominators are in play. Against an unpadded circuit of 220 constraints, nF,bl = 123 136
at d = 128 is ≈ 11.7% (and 106 752 at d = 64 is ≈ 10.2%); against the padded nF = 221 it is ≈ 5.9% (resp.
≈ 5.1%). The percentages quoted elsewhere in the paper refer to the first.
   The distinction matters because the block is affordable only when it fits inside padding the circuit already
carries: a circuit of exactly 220 constraints does not leave room, and appending the block there pushes m
past the power of two and doubles it, a 100% overhead rather than an 11% one. The sets above, stated
at nF = 221 , therefore describe a circuit whose proper size lies between 925 441 and 221 − nF,bl = 1 974 016
constraints at d = 128. The block should accordingly be reserved by the frontend at circuit-synthesis time, not
appended afterwards. Two levers shrink it: taking b = 4 (item (5) of §5) divides the requirement by log2 6,
giving nF,bl = 47 744, about 4.6% of 220 , and halves k at the same time; failing that, regime (2) of Lemma 3.3
needs nR,bl ≈ 45 at d = 128, about 0.5%, at the price of a computational rather than statistical hiding term.
Remark 4.21 (The mask-block commitment). Everything above is a per-call sweep over the eight batched
invocations of a folding step, all of which run at ℓ = 16. The mask-block commitment Cy,0 of Protocol 7 is not
                                                                                                          n
among them, and we cannot take it monolithically. By Remark B.3 it carries the pair (cy , xy ) ∈ RκF × RF R,in
in BDLOP slots, so a single commitment would need a message length κ + nR,in , which equals 16 only by
coincidence. At d = 64 we have κ = 19 > 16, so this exceeds ℓ already at nR,in = 0, while at d = 128, κ = 11
leaves five free slots and it exceeds ℓ as soon as nR,in > 5.
    The obstruction is not the mismatch itself but its shape: κ + nR,in depends on the circuit. A monolithic
mask block would therefore not have a parameter set at all, but a family of them, one per input length,
together with the salt width m2 + κ + nR,in − ℓ that preserving the MLWE secret dimension forces alongside
it; and no finite number of estimator runs covers a family. We avoid this by splitting the message rather
than widening the commitment.
    Fix the public partition of [κ + nR,in ] into
                                                                  
                                               Ñ := (κ + nR,in )/ℓ
                                                                                        (ñ)                    (ñ)        (ñ)   
consecutive blocks of ℓ slots, the last zero-padded, and commit to the ñ-th block as Cy,0 := Commit m(ñ) , s1,y,0 , s2,y,0
under its own fresh salt pair, all Ñ pairs being disjoint from the salts of {Cy,j }. Step 6 of Protocol 7 sends the
          (ñ)
tuple (Cy,0 )ñ∈[Ñ ] , Step 12 opens all Ñ of them, and V runs its recomputation and its norm check blockwise
before reassembling (cy , xy ) by concatenation. Nothing else changes. The partition is public and fixed before
any challenge is drawn, so the binding argument of Remark B.9 applies blockwise and pins the concatenation;
and no compatibility constraint is violated, since Cy,0 is never combined homomorphically with any other
commitment (V’s Step 21 check involves only {Cy,j }j∈[t] , and cy enters the folded c through its opened value).
   Every block is then an ABDLOP commitment at the ambient (ℓ, m1 , m2 ) under the ambient matrices
(A1 , A2 , B), with three consequences.
    (1) Hiding is MLWEκ+ℓ, m2 −κ−ℓ and binding is MSISκ, m +m , 2B̃ √(m +m )d . Note also that the blocks
                                                                     1   2       1   2
         carry native RF -vectors.
    (2) As recorded in Remark B.9, this is the one commitment whose salts are opened, and the count is
         therefore over differences on the revealed-salt set. At B̃ − 1 = 1 two accepting openings of a block
         differ by a vector with coefficients in {−2, . . . , 2}, of which there are at most 5(m1 +m2 )d ; and since
         q = 264 − 59 has multiplicative order 32 modulo 128 and 64 modulo 256, the polynomial X d + 1 splits

4.4. The accumulator-free variant. In Protocol 12 the integer k needs to handle two unrelated roles: it
counts the accumulator pairs the prover samples at Step 1, and it is the number of pieces Π′DEC outputs.
These two roles are tied together by our desire to provide a blueprint for a zero-knowledge folding scheme, as
outlined in Protocol 13; the sketched protocol ΠLBF−Fold aims to feed round r’s output back as round r + 1’s
optional input, and ΠSuperNeo ’s own ΠRLC likewise folds K fresh instances against k accumulators. However,
neither duty is a hiding one and the first can be dropped outright.
   Indeed, hiding in Appendix B.4 is carried by the Gaussian mask y of Protocol 7 and, for c1 , by the blinding
block of Definition 3.1. The k sampled pairs enter the simulator of Protocol 19 only as data S generates
itself with the honest sampler and on which it then runs the honest prover; none of the hybrid simulators
                                                                 zk
H0 –H6 charges anything for them, and they are absent from NPoK      = 4 + 2t for exactly that reason.
   Write kin for the number of sampled input accumulators, so that Protocol 12 is the case kin = k, and let
Π◦LBF be the protocol obtained at kin = 0: Step 1 is deleted, Π′R1CS and Π′RLC run on the single fresh pair
alone, so that v = ρ1 z1 and z = v + y, while Π′DEC still outputs k pieces.

Proof. Lemmas 4.9, 4.10 and 4.12 are stated for an arbitrary number K + kin of input pairs and nowhere
