Three consequences follow, each proportional to where k or kin appears. First, the public part of the sampled
pairs vanishes from the transcript: the ≈ 1.15 MB at d = 128 (resp. ≈ 0.80 MB at d = 64) that §4.3.3.1
charges to the two “sampled input” rows is removed outright, together with the kin t ABDLOP commitments
and the one transmitted instance xblind . Second, every remaining k-indexed family shrinks by the ratio
26/31 (resp. 25/30), bringing a folding step to roughly 6 MB at d = 128 and 4 MB at d = 64 on the same
accounting. Third, on the soundness side εcw = Wmax (K +kin )/|C| improves from ≈ 2−116 to 2−120 at d = 64,
so the term that item 1 of Remark 4.19 identifies as limiting that instantiation to ≈ 116 bits is no longer
below λ = 120.
58                                                LATTICEBLINDFOLD


Remark 4.25 (Why the pairs are kept in Protocol 12). The pairs are kept in Protocol 12 for interface
compatibility and by our desire to provide a blueprint for a zero-knowledge folding scheme, as outlined in
Protocol 13. Π◦LBF still outputs an element of CEcom (b, L, B̃, Commit, T, K)k , but its input relation is CCS◦ (b)
alone and has no slot for an incoming accumulator. The second branch of Protocol 13 is therefore unavailable
to it, it cannot be iterated, and it cannot be substituted for ΠRLC inside a ΠSuperNeo pipeline, whose input
type is CEcom (b, L, B̃, Commit)K+k . What ΠLBF buys with its k sampled pairs is precisely a step whose input
and output types match, at the cost of the five decomposition digits above.
   For the one-shot use case this paper is primarily aimed at, namely wrapping a single R1CS circuit in
zero-knowledge (§1.2.1), Π◦LBF is strictly the better object and the figures of this section are the operative
ones. For the blueprint of §4.2.1, the sampled pairs are what make the composition type-correct. All concrete
figures reported elsewhere in the paper (§4.3.3.1, §4.3.4) are stated at kin = k, i.e. for ΠLBF .

4.5. A comment about Fiat–Shamir. Every figure in §4.3.4 is an interactive one, and the interactive
statements are the ones proved in this paper. We record here what changes non-interactively, writing Q
for the bound on the number of random-oracle queries. The change is not cosmetic: at q = 264 − 59 the
quadratic extension K = Fq2 used throughout does not suffice at any Q of cryptographic interest, and the
deployment path requires K = Fq4 . We state this here rather than in §5 because it is a requirement on the
parameters, not an open problem.
    Q counts random-oracle queries, not interactions, and it is the reason a non-interactive figure is not the
interactive one: a non-interactive proof is produced offline, so nobody rate-limits the prover, which may
rehash as often as its budget allows. Two conventions are defensible. Under the conservative one, λ bits
against adversaries running in time 2λ means log2 Q = λ, so the interactive errors must be ≈ 2−2λ ; this
is the usual square-root phenomenon and the reading we recommend for a deployment target. Under the
budget convention one fixes Q by a plausible hashing budget, in which case a public proof system should still
not assume Q ≤ 264 . We report every requirement as a function of λ + log2 Q so that either can be read off,
and take Q = 280 as the default point. One caveat cuts the other way and we record it without relying on
it: a query here is not one hash, since a fresh ρ
                                                ⃗ costs a fresh ABDLOP mask commitment and a re-ground
Sum-Check challenge costs a round polynomial, which buys back perhaps 20–30 bits.
    Assume the round-by-round property discussed below, so that the loss is a multiplicative O(Q) on each
knowledge-soundness term. Evaluating at the d = 128 set (k = 31, t = 3, K = 1, log2 m = 21, Dmax = 4)
with λ = 120 and Q = 280 :

          term                        interactive   requirement for λ bits under FS   at λ + log2 Q = 200
                                         −121.6                           λ+log2 Q
          εSC = log2 (m)Dmax /|K|      2             |K| ≥ log2 (m)Dmax 2                  |K| ≥ 2206.4
          εSZ (tensored challenges)    2−123.5       |K| ≥ (log2 (m) + 2) 2λ+log2 Q        |K| ≥ 2204.5
          εcw = Q(K + k)/|C|            2−243           |C| ≥ (K + k) 2λ+log2 Q             |C| ≥ 2205
            (1) (1)                                   (1)                      (1)     (1)
          NPoK εABDLOP                 2−140.9       εABDLOP ≤ 2−λ−log2 Q /NPoK       εABDLOP ≤ 2−206.7

   Three consequences follow, in decreasing order of what they cost to fix. The extension degree must
rise: at q = 264 − 59, |Fq2 | = 2128 falls short by some 78 bits and |Fq3 | = 2192 by 14.4, sufficing only
for log2 Q ≤ 65.6, whereas |Fq4 | = 2256 clears the requirement with 50 bits to spare and clears even the
conservative convention, which asks for 2246.4 . Remark 3.17 records that §3.3 generalises to e = 4 without
new hypotheses; at fixed ℓ the packed block counts, and hence the proof size, scale by e/2 = 2, which is the
honest price of a non-interactive instantiation at these parameters. |C| is already large enough at d = 128
and is not at d = 64: with |C| = 4d one has 2256 against the required 2205 at d = 128, but only 2128 at
d = 64, which is what rules that set out non-interactively (Remark 4.26). |CABDLOP | must be widened: the
                    (1)
estimator returns εABDLOP ≈ 2/|CABDLOP | at challenge coefficients bounded by 2, against a requirement of
|CABDLOP | ≳ 2208 ; since CABDLOP has roughly d/2 + 1 = 65 free coefficients at d = 128, raising that bound
from 2 to about 7 suffices. This last is a knob rather than an obstruction, but it widens η and hence s1 , s2
and MPoK , and the whole set must be re-run through the estimator; we have not done so.
   For a multi-round public-coin protocol the Fiat–Shamir loss is not additive in general, and ΠLBF has
log2 (m) = 21 Sum-Check rounds, the challenges α, γ, ζ, ρ  ⃗, µ
                                                              ⃗ , and the internal challenge rounds of NPoK
ABDLOP invocations; the generic multi-round bound would give a vacuous Qµ degradation. The table
above assumes instead the O(Q) loss that round-by-round soundness would deliver, and we record this
                                             LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                59


as an assumption rather than a theorem: one must prove that each sub-protocol invoked is round-by-
round (equivalently, state-restoration) knowledge sound. We have not verified this for the Sum-Check-plus-
ABDLOP composition, and regard it as the main gap between the interactive statement proved here and
the non-interactive target (item (2) of §5); see Remark B.10 for the separate question of the simulator under
Fiat–Shamir.
Remark 4.26 (Grinding on the mask loop under Fiat–Shamir). One term degrades in a way specific to
this protocol. The coordinate-wise extraction loss εcw = Wmax (K + k)/|C| of Proposition 4.17 is inflated by
Wmax precisely because Wmax bounds the number of challenge blocks ρ    ⃗ a malicious prover may select among
before completing an attempt (Remark 4.3). Interactively this is enforced by V, which resamples ρ     ⃗ afresh
each iteration; under Fiat–Shamir there is no verifier to enforce it and a prover may rehash freely, so Wmax
must be replaced by Q, giving εFScw = Q(K + k)/|C|.
   This is immaterial at d = 128, where |C| ≈ 2256 gives ≈ 2−171 at Q = 280 , and fatal at d = 64,
where |C| ≈ 2128 gives ≈ 2−43 . The d = 64 instantiation, already only ≈ 116 bits interactively (item 1
of Remark 4.19), is therefore not viable non-interactively at any query bound of cryptographic interest; the
remedy is again a larger C, at |C| ≥ (K + k)2λ+log2 Q rather than 2λ . This cuts against the observation in
Remark 2.12 that the attempt caps may be lifted under Fiat–Shamir: lifting them is free for completeness,
but the cap’s role in εcw is a knowledge-soundness one, and there it is replaced by Q, not removed.

                           5. Future work and open research directions
   As mentioned in §4.3.3, we expect LatticeBlindFold to admit substantially better performance under
further optimization. We collect below the theoretical frameworks and concrete optimizations we regard as
most promising, in decreasing order of expected impact, and leave comparing and benchmarking them to
future work.
    (1) Abort-free masking via a Hint-MLWE argument. The work [KLSS23] drops rejection sampling en-
        tirely, masking at a width only a small constant times ∥secret∥2 and arguing simulatability com-
        putationally, on the strength of a reduction from Hint-MLWE to standard MLWE. Adopting it
        here would remove the attempt budgets altogether, and with them both the completeness error of
                                   √
        Proposition 4.15 and the nF inflation of the norm bound that forces k = Θ(log nF ) (Remark 4.18).
        Since that inflation is the dominant structural cost of blinding, this is the single change most likely
        to close the gap with ΠSuperNeo , and we regard it as the most promising direction the paper leaves
        open.
    (2) A decider for the output claims. As recorded in §4.3.3, ΠLBF stops at k committed evaluation claims
        plus their ABDLOP openings, and a deployable zero-knowledge wrapper needs something that checks
        them succinctly. The natural candidate is to arithmetize the ABDLOP verification, since its linear
        part is already R1CS-shaped over RF and the componentwise instantiation of §3.3 keeps it over the
        base ring; the obstruction is the norm checks, which want the exact ℓ2 certificate of [LNP22, §5.2]
        rather than the relaxed bounds a single invocation gets away with. Solving it would also unblock the
        recursive branch of Protocol 13, the two needing the same certificate (Remark 4.8), and we regard
        it as the main missing piece between this paper and a deployable implementation.
    (3) Round-by-round knowledge soundness: the paper’s main open proof obligation. The non-interactive
        figures of §4.5 all assume the O(Q) Fiat–Shamir loss that round-by-round soundness would buy;
        with log2 (m) = 21 Sum-Check rounds plus the internal challenges of NPoK ≈ 8 batched [LNP22]
        invocations, the generic multi-round bound is vacuous, so this is a theorem to prove, not a parameter
        to tune. It must be established for the composition, including the Wmax -capped mask loop of
        Protocol 7, whose cap has no non-interactive enforcer (Remark 4.26). The simulator side is a separate
        obligation, Remark B.10 being a ROM statement obtained by programming hash per attempt. By
        contrast the extension degree is a parameter choice: the quartic set should be produced and estimated
        with the implementation now in progress, ℓ re-tuned as Remark 3.17 suggests and CABDLOP widened
        accordingly. Until then the non-interactive figures are conditional and the interactive ones are the
        results of this paper.
    (4) Report core-SVP alongside the root Hermite factor. We follow [LNP22, §6.1] in measuring lattice
        hardness by the root Hermite factor δ < 1.0045, which keeps our figures comparable with theirs
        but is a coarse proxy at the margins we claim. A current estimator should be run on the three
60                                                  LATTICEBLINDFOLD


         instances that matter, namely the Ajtai MSIS at 4T B, the ABDLOP MSIS at the extracted bound
         of Lemma 3.6 evaluated at the widest call, and the MLWE instance, with core-SVP costs reported
         for each; binding at βrlx needs no run of its own, being dominated by the second.
     (5) Defer and batch every proof of knowledge. Soundness is anchored by the commitments, which are
         sent at the right times, so the proofs of knowledge about them can all be deferred and batched in
         the style of [LNP22]. That would take ≈ 7 invocations down to 2, cutting both the round count√and
         the additive κcom (m1 + m2 )d communication terms roughly threefold, at the mild price of a Nc
         growth in the batched masking width. Combined with the deduplication of Remark 4.14, this is the
         most direct saving available without changing the protocol’s structure.
     (6) The decomposition basis as a knob, and a parameter sweep. Taking b = 4 halves k while doubling
         the Sum-Check degree, which is roughly a wash on the prover since k · Dmax is invariant, but halves
         the k-multiplied ABDLOP, proof-of-knowledge and communication terms, and ktd in εSZ with them.
         More generally, once the implementation now in progress is available, the illustrative points should
         be replaced by a sweep over (b, d, ξ, nF , q, T (C)); several choices we made for analytical convenience,
         such as ξ ≈ 20 and a quadratic K, are plausibly not optimal.
     (7) Higher-degree extensions, and binary fields. Given the recent interest in towers of binary fields,
         beginning with [DP23] and continuing with [Set26, BRW26], it would be interesting to investigate
         protocols of this shape over extensions of much higher degree than the quadratic K = Fq2 we use.
         Several of our choices are degree-dependent, among them the componentwise instantiation of §3.3,
                                    (ct)
         the single execution of Πmany , and the εSZ , εSC terms, so the trade-off is not simply a matter of
         enlarging |K|; and the lattice layer has no characteristic-2 analogue that we know of.
   Several narrower questions are left open along the way: whether blinding extends to K ≥ 2, whether
a Lova-style analogue can be built over Z/264 Z, and whether the rejection-sampling requirements can be
relaxed to admit smaller primes. In the end, we plan to implement the protocol and include detailed
benchmarks.

         suggesting this line of research, and for their insightful comments and advice throughout the work, which
         led to significant improvements and resolved key issues. Moreover, the author is grateful to Antoine Douchet,
         Khalil Gibran, Sebastien Guillemot, and Srinath Setty (in alphabetical order) for several fruitful discussions.

the author, who takes full responsibility for the correctness and originality of the work.

Note that, the initial consistency check in Protocol 6 is necessary for obtaining a strong interactive reduction;
cf. Remark 2.29. The extractor constructed in the proof of [LNP22, Lemma 3.1] manages to extract a solution
to the MSIS problem if the prover P ∗ passes with a non-negligible probability. Such probability is contained
in the relaxed success probability ϵ′ (A, P ∗ ).
   Now, since we are enforcing the ZKPoK checks at Steps 7 and 18 (we require them to link the evaluation
to the correct execution of the Sum-Check protocol and then call its soundness, cf. Remark 4.2), we can
then proceed as in [NS26, Appendix B.2], mutatis mutandis. The resulting knowledge-soundness error is the
sum

Sum-Check protocol itself, and the blinding challenge ζ), the second group is contributed by our modifications
(Lemmas 3.13, 3.8), and the last group by the ABDLOP proofs of knowledge of Steps 1, 7, 12, 15 and 18.
   Let us stress that εSZ is not inherited from SuperNeo. Loc. cit. batches with the powers of a single challenge
and separates the three families by further powers γ K , γ 2K+k ; we batch with the tensored challenges of Step 2

B.2. Proof of Lemma 4.10. We prove here Lemma 4.10. Before diving into the proof we state and prove

   We can now dive into the proof of the lemma.
   We begin by showing that the protocol is complete, then that it is public coin, and in the end we show
the properties of weak interactive reduction.
   Completeness: By Lemma B.1, Lemma 3.20 and Lemma 2.25, an honest execution that leaves the loop
produces an output in the target relation. Two distinct failure modes remain, and they must be charged
separately.
   The first is the mask loop itself. The algorithm is still PPT and concludes with ≈ 2, 3 repetitions (of
the first sampling lines) in expectation, and the attempt cap of Protocol 7 truncates only the tail of this
distribution. More precisely, by Lemma 3.20 and the independence of the masks y drawn across attempts,
an honest prover fails to obtain an accepting attempt within Wmax iterations with probability at most
(1 − 1/M )Wmax ≤ 2−λ , exactly as an Rej1 abort is charged in [LNP22]; cf. Remark 4.3.
                                                                                  (1)
   The second is the ABDLOP proofs of knowledge, which Protocol Πmany ((s2 , s1 , m), (f1 , f2 , . . . , fN )) per-
                                                                 −λ                                                PoK
forms with abort, each is complete only with error 2 , under its own, independent, retry budget Wmax                   .

whence (K + k)t + t of them. These budgets are nested inside the mask loop but do not interact with it. A
proof-of-knowledge retry resamples only (y1 , y2 ) and the challenge c of that sub-protocol, never the mask y,
the mask commitments Cy,0 , {Cy,j }, or the folding challenges ρ     ⃗, so no proof-of-knowledge retry can be used
to obtain a second draw of ρ⃗.

   Public Coin: The proof of knowledge protocol in Protocol Πmany ((s2 , s1 , m), (f1 , f2 , . . . , fN )) is public
coin, and the verifier’s randomness consists of challenges ρ1 , . . . , ρk+K , which are sampled uniformly at
random from C and sent to the prover.
   Weak Interactive Reduction:
   As in [NS26, Appendix B.3] we make use of Lemma 2.19, but before proceeding with it, we must record
a couple of important remarks on why we can still apply this lemma.
   The mask y can be treated as fixed under the rewind: the mask commitments for a given attempt are
always sent before the corresponding ρ1 , . . . , ρK+k are sampled, so the protocol’s public-coin structure forces
any malicious prover to fix its mask commitments before it has any information about the challenges it is
about to be rewound on. Below, we make this precise by building the fixed mask prefix explicitly into the
state st∗ passed to the adversary function A(pp,s,u1 ,st∗ ) , rather than relying on the single-round formulation
of the adversary function in [FMN23, Figure 11], which has no inner rejection loop and so leaves this point
implicit. Once the mask is fixed, the witnesses z1 , . . . , zK+k are recovered exactly as in [NS26, Appendix B.3]
via Lemma 2.19. More precisely, this holds true as all quantities are now shifted by the same y and the
same salts. The mask y itself, together with cy , xy , {yy,j }, is recovered separately, from the final, accepting
transcript; since P ∗ passes the commitment check then we can proceed as in the proof of Lemma 3.6
(see [LNP22, Lemma 3.1]) and define a EPT extractor E ′ which extracts the corresponding salts.
66                                               LATTICEBLINDFOLD


   Following Definition 2.6, we consider an arbitrary EPT adversary (A, P ∗ ) for Π′RLC with success probability,
ϵ(A, P ∗ ) ≥ 1/poly(λ). In order to precisely define the adversary function, we need to modify the single-round
setting of [FMN23, Figure 11], since we must handle the Rej1 while-loop. The adversary and verification
functions below are therefore parametrised by a transcript prefix
                                                                         
                                            trpre := Cy,0 , {Cy,j }j∈[t]
together with P ∗ ’s internal state stP ∗ at the point where that prefix was sent (its random tape, folded in via
an averaging argument over EPT provers). We write st∗ := (stP ∗ , trpre ) and treat it as fixed under all the
rewinding that follows: trpre , and hence y and its associated salts and commitments, are never resampled.
How st∗ is generated is a separate question, deferred to Protocol 17; we note here only that it is not obtained
by running P ∗ until its first Rej1 -accepting attempt, for the reason given after that protocol. Lemma 2.19
applies to every fixed st∗ alike, so nothing in the present paragraph depends on the choice.
   We can now set the machinery of Lemma 2.19 in motion, by constructing an adversary function A(pp,s,u1 ,st∗ )
and a verification function V(pp,s,u1 ,st∗ ) as in Protocol 15 and Protocol 16 respectively. We highlight in green
all changes from [NS26, Appendix B.3].

does it invoke E(pp, s, u1 , st∗ ). The prefix is thus drawn afresh in each of the N iterations, and is not the
prefix of the first accepting attempt; see the discussion following Protocol 17.

We highlight two main differences from [NS26, Appendix B.3], both forced by the Rej1 loop. First, the
attempt index τ is sampled uniformly from [Wmax ] rather than taken to be the first accepting attempt.
Conditioning on acceptance biases the prefix distribution towards prefixes on which P ∗ is unusually coop-
erative, and no union bound over attempts would then be available. Second, the outer loop amplifies. The
membership test in the penultimate step is decidable in polynomial time from (pp, s, u1 , w1 ), consisting only
of norm bounds and linear commitment identities, which is what makes amplification implementable.
   Because st∗ fixes trpre = (Cy,0 , {Cy,j }j∈[t] ), the mask y and its salts and commitments are identical across
every branch A(pp,s,u1 ,st∗ ) (⃗c) we rewind to, by construction. In particular, the opening (cy , xy ), s1,y,0 , s2,y,0
of Cy,0 produced at the end of each such branch is the same across branches, by binding of Commit (cf.
Remark B.9). Both components matter and for different clauses of Definition 3.21: cy cancels in L(z − z(i) )
and xy in Lin (z − z(i) ), so that the extracted zi satisfies ci = L(zi ) and xi = Lin (zi ). Had xy been left
as an unbound post-challenge message, the second of these would fail with no extractable contradiction; cf.
Remark B.3.
   We already modified the adversary A(pp,s,u1 ,st∗ ) , so we must relate the accepting probability of V(pp,s,u1 ,st∗ )
to ϵ(A, P ∗ ). Lemma 3.20 is not available for this purpose as it describes the honest sampling procedure,
whereas P ∗ need neither draw y from DsnF nor evaluate Rej1 faithfully, and may decline to complete an
attempt whenever the challenge block it has just received is unfavourable. Such issue motivates the in-
troduction of the attempt budget Wmax . Fix θ := (pp, s, u1 , st) and write p(θ) for the conditional success
probability of ⟨P ∗ , V⟩, so that ϵ(A, P ∗ ) = Eθ [p(θ)]. For τ ∈ [Wmax ] let Reachτ be the event that P ∗ initiates
a τ -th attempt, let st∗τ be the corresponding prefix, and put
                                  pτ (st∗τ ) := Pr V(pp,s,u1 ,st∗τ ) ⃗c, A(pp,s,u1 ,st∗τ ) (⃗c) = 1 .

At this point we discuss why we needed to introduce N . Conditioned on θ, the N iterations of the outer
loop use independent randomness and the membership test of the penultimate step is efficiently decidable,
68                                                         LATTICEBLINDFOLD


so the extractor fails only if every iteration fails, an event of probability at most (1 − a(θ))N . If u(θ) ≤ 0
there is nothing to prove, the left-hand side of the display below being non-negative; assume therefore
u(θ) > 0. We set N = λ2 Wmax , which is polynomial in λ by Definition 2.2. At the working level λ = 120
and with Wmax ≤ 28 at every instantiation (namely Wmax = 206 for M ≈ 3 and Wmax = 122 for M ≈ 2, cf.
Remark 4.3), this is N ≤ 221.8 . Since a(θ) ≥ u(θ)/Wmax we have N a(θ) ≥ λ2 u(θ), whence
                                    N                               2
                       1 − 1 − a(θ)      ≥ 1 − e−N a(θ) ≥ 1 − e−λ u(θ) ≥ u(θ) − e−λ .
For the last inequality write x := λ2 u(θ) and distinguish three cases. If u(θ) ≤ λ−2 then x ≤ 1, and 1−e−x ≥
x/2 = λ2 u(θ)/2 ≥ u(θ) for λ ≥ 2. If λ−2 < u(θ) ≤ λ−1 then x > 1, so 1 − e−x > 1 − e−1 > λ−1 ≥ u(θ). If
u(θ) > λ−1 then x > λ, so 1 − e−x > 1 − e−λ ≥ u(θ) − e−λ . Only this last case consumes the additive slack,
and it does so by e−λ , which is negligible. Averaging over θ and using Eθ [u(θ)] = ϵ(A, P ∗ ) − Wmax (K + k)/|C|
yields extraction probability at least ϵ(A, P ∗ ) − Wmax (K + k)/|C| − negl(λ). By Definition 2.2, the quantities
Wmax , K and k are polynomial in λ while 1/|C| = negl(λ), so Wmax (K + k)/|C| = negl(λ) and Definition 2.6
is satisfied. Finally, N is fixed in advance and independent of the run, so linearity of expectation bounds
the expected running time of E by N times that of a single iteration, each of which is EPT; E is therefore
EPT. We stress that it is the fixed bound N that makes this argument available. An outer loop repeated
until success would have expected cost ≈ 1/a(θ), which no protocol parameter bounds (cf. Remark B.5).
   We can then proceed as in [NS26, Appendix B.3], recalling that the ABDLOP commitment scheme is
linear in both the salts and the message, and deduce that the extractor E(pp, s, u1 , st) is EPT, succeeding in
extracting witnesses

                                                                                                      com (q/2, L, βrlx , Commit)

       ∥∆i sil,j ∥2   ≤   ∥c̄i (c̄ sl,j )∥2 + ∥c̄(c̄i sl,j )∥2   ≤   T1 (C) ∥c̄ sl,j ∥2 + ∥c̄i sl,j ∥2    ≤ 2T1 (C)βrlx = βrlx ,
as required. The three factors of ∆i are precisely the two c̄’s of Lemma 3.6(2) and the one challenge difference
of Lemma 2.19.The witness-norm clause of R′2 remains the vacuous q/2 inherited from [NS26, Appendix B.3],
since coordinate-wise inversion certifies no bound on zi ; that bound is supplied instead, within the same
round, by the norm-check polynomial NC(X)       ⃗ of Protocol 6, Step 3 (cf. Remark B.4.(1)).
   For the second requirement of a weak interactive reduction, fix A = (B, B ′ ) as in Definition 2.6 and two
instances u1 , u′1 with ϕ(u1 ) = ϕ(u′1 ), i.e. agreeing on (ci )i∈[K+k] . Since ψ retains only the zi , the claim to
be proved is that the two extractions agree on those, and this is the argument of [NS26, Appendix B.3]
verbatim: ci = L(zi ) together with the relaxed binding of L pins zi , up to ϵ(B, C).
   We stress that the corresponding statement for the full witness is false, which is why ψ is needed. Neither
remaining component is a function of ϕ(u1 ). The hints satisfy yji = M̄    ]
                                                                             j zi (r) and r is part of u1 , not of ϕ(u1 ),
so two instances agreeing under ϕ but carrying distinct evaluation points yield distinct hints; and the salts
extracted at Steps 1 and 17 are the ones P ∗ freshly sampled in that run, on which nothing in u1 imposes
a constraint beyond the commitment identity itself. An alternative repair would be to enlarge ϕ so as to
determine them, but ϕ would then have to include r and the Ci,j , and clause (1) of Definition 2.7 would have
to be re-established for Π′R1CS against that larger ϕ, which fails for the same reason: r′ is verifier randomness
and the output commitments carry fresh salts, so two runs do not agree on them.
   The knowledge-soundness error is
                                                                                              (1)
                εRLC ≤ εcw + ϵ(B, C) + ϵ(Commit, βrlx , C) + ϵbind (B̃) + (K + k)t + t εABDLOP ,
the sum of the coordinate-wise extraction loss Wmax (K + k)/|C| of Lemma 2.19 inflated by the attempt
budget, the (2B, C)-relaxed-binding error of the Ajtai commitment scheme L, the binding error of the
ABDLOP commitment scheme Commit at the relaxed bound βrlx (Definition 3.22) and the strong sampling
set C, the B̃-binding error arising from the two-opening argument for Cy,0 (Remark B.9), and the knowledge-
soundness error of the (K + k)t + t ABDLOP proofs of knowledge of Steps 1 and 17, through which the
                                              LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                  69


extractor E ′ above recovers the salts. Their sum is negligible in λ (cf. also Proposition 4.17), concluding the
proof as in [NS26, Appendix B.3].
Remark B.2 (Why the loop is capped). The bound Wmax of Protocol 7 is necessary for proving soundness.
Without it, an EPT P ∗ admits only a bound E[W ] ≤ W̄ on its expected number of attempts, so truncating
the analysis at a budget pλ,W := p(λ)W̄ leaves a residue Pr[W > pλ,W ] ≤ 1/p(λ) by Markov’s inequality,
which is an inverse polynomial, not a negligible function. With the cap, Pr[W > Wmax ] = 0 by construction,
and Definitions 2.6 and 2.7 are used exactly as in [NS26, Definitions 16–17], with Proposition 2.8 applied
(almost) verbatim.
   The price is a completeness error of 2−λ per capped loop. The same argument, and the same price, apply
                               PoK
verbatim to the retry budget Wmax  (Equation (B.1)) of the ABDLOP proofs of knowledge, which we assume to
be enforced through each proof of knowledge. There too an uncapped loop would leave an inverse-polynomial
Markov residue in the union bound over which attempt a malicious prover elects to complete. Since our
protocols cap 1 + NPoK loops in all, the total is the εLBF
                                                       c   of Proposition 4.15.
   We stress that these caps bound the number of attempts an honest prover makes and the number of first
messages a malicious one may select among. However, they do not bound, and must not be used to bound,
the number of times an extractor rewinds. That count is governed by the adversary’s success probability, and
the rewinding loops of Protocol 18 are accordingly left uncapped and analysed in expectation; cf. Remark B.5.
Remark B.3 (xy must be bound before ρ           ⃗, not merely sent after it). We cannot leave xy = Lin (y) as a
plain (post-challenge) message even though it is public data that the verifier folds into x and never checks a
norm on. The problem arises as it would break the extraction argument of Lemma 4.10.
                                                                                                  (i)
    The extractor rewinds to two branches differing only in coordinate i and forms zi := (ρi − ρi )−1 (z − z(i) ).
For the commitment clause of Definition 3.21 this is enough: cy is fixed across branches by binding of
                                                                     (i)
Commit, so it cancels in the difference and L(z − z(i) ) = (ρi − ρi )ci . For the instance clause xi = Lin (zi )
it is not, since
                                                            (i)
                                  Lin (z − z(i) ) = (ρi − ρi )xi + xy − xy(i) ,
                                                                               
                                                                                   P
and an unbound xy may be chosen per branch, indeed defined as Lin (z) − j ρj xj after the fact. Two
branches that disagree there do not yield a contradiction to extract from. Both z and z(i) are then short
preimages of different targets, so no MSIS solution is produced and the extractor simply fails.
    Committing to the pair (cy , xy ) at Step 6 removes the issue at once. Binding of Commit fixes xy before
                                                                                              (i)
⃗ is drawn, exactly as it already fixes cy , and the displayed difference collapses to (ρi − ρi )xi . The cost is
ρ
nR,in further BDLOP message slots on the one commitment Cy,0 , i.e. nR,in d log2 q bits on its tB component;
the values themselves are free, xy being already sent in the clear at Step 12. It would be wrong, however,
to read this as leaving the transmitted opening untouched. The message (cy , xy ) has length κ + nR,in , which
overflows the ℓ message slots of a single commitment, so Cy,0 is in fact a tuple of Ñ = ⌈(κ + nR,in )/ℓ⌉
commitments, each carrying its own salt pair into the revealed opening; see Remark 4.21.
    Similarly, sending xy in the clear before ρ    ⃗ is not an acceptable alternative. In a rejected attempt the
conditional law of y depends on v, and hence on the witness, which is precisely the leak Remark B.9 exists
to prevent; the same objection that forces cy under a commitment forces xy under one too.
    The analogous question for {Cy,j }j∈[t] does not arise. There the homomorphic check of Step 21, together
with binding of Commit, already pins yy,j before ρ     ⃗, and the hint clause of Definition 3.21 then follows by
RF -linearity in the difference exactly as the commitment clause does.
B.3. Proof of Lemma 4.12. Before proving Lemma 4.12, let us single out two structural considerations.
Remark B.4.
    (1) Π′DEC performs no independent check that each extracted piece zi satisfies ∥zi ∥∞ < b. This bound is
        guaranteed because splitb is applied to an already norm-bounded z by an honest prover, and is other-
        wise enforced downstream by the norm-check polynomial NC(X)     ⃗ of Π′
                                                                               R1CS ’s Sum-Check (Protocol 6,
        Step 3). ΠDEC is therefore sound only as a component of the specific composition Π′DEC ◦ Π′RLC ◦ Π′R1CS
                   ′

        analyzed in Theorem 4.13, and should not be treated as an independently reusable building block
        without re-deriving norm-bound enforcement for its new context.
    (2) Π′DEC is not deterministic. Step 5 carries a verifier challenge and the extractor rewinds it.
70                                                      LATTICEBLINDFOLD

Three points complete the argument:
    (i) E is EPT: We condition on the first execution being accepting with first message (w, v), and write
ε(w,v) := Prc [V accepts | (w, v)]. The number of rewinds is then geometric with parameter ε(w,v) , of
expectation ε−1    (w,v) , while the branch is reached at all only with probability ε(w,v) . Averaging over first
messages gives an expected number of rewinds at most 1, uniformly in ϵ(A, P ∗ ). This is the standard
rewinding argument underlying Lemma 3.6, which we invoke rather than re-derive. The bound is uniform
in ϵ(A, P ∗ ), which is what the composition needs; see Remark B.5. The failure probability of this step is
 (1)                                                 PoK                                PoK
εABDLOP , which already carries the factor Wmax           accounting for the at most Wmax   first messages a malicious
prover may select among before completing an attempt (§4.3.2).
    (ii) w2 is the same across the rewound branches: since u2 is fixed at Step 3 of Protocol 8 and binding of
L and Commit pins the openings; this costs the term ϵ(Commit, B̃, C).
    (iii) (s; u1 ; w1 ) ∈ CErlx
                                                                                                         P i−1
                            com (B, L, βrlx , Commit): the recompositions are correct by Lemma 3.6(3), ∥    ib   zi ∥ ∞ <
B by R2 -membership of w2 , and the salt clause is the relaxed one supplied by Lemma 3.6(2). This is the
relation in which Protocol 16 accepts, so the branch witnesses handed to Lemma 2.19 are of the right type;
cf. Remark 3.23 and, for the scope of the relaxation, Remark 4.8.

Remark B.5. For the sake of clarity, let us explain why the extractor does not need to be capped. Wmax
of Equation B.1 is the honest prover’s rejection-sampling retry cap inside an ABDLOP proof of knowledge.
It is public, enforced by V, and its purpose is to make budget exhaustion a witness-independent event of
probability at most 2−λ , charged to completeness (Remark 4.3, Proposition 4.15). The number of rewinds
an extractor needs is a different quantity altogether. it is governed by ϵ(A, P ∗ ), which no protocol parameter
                                       PoK
bounds. Capping the rewinding at Wmax       would establish knowledge soundness only against provers of success
                   PoK
probability ≳ 1/Wmax   , i.e. it would establish nothing.
   Note that the cap is not needed to compose. In the knowledge-soundness proof extraction runs backwards,
so the adversary A that Lemma 2.19 rewinds on the folding coefficients ⃗c is the one that runs P ∗ through Pro-
tocol 8 and applies EDEC to the result(this is how it produces the branch witness w2 whose CErlx com -membership
Protocol 16 checks). That lemma asks only that A be EPT, which point (i) of the proof of Lemma 4.12 in
§B.3 supplies. The two randomnesses are independent (the coins EDEC spends rewinding the Step 5 challenge
                                               LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                  71


have nothing to do with ⃗c) so the expected total work is the product of the two expectations (Wald’s identity),
both polynomial.
   The cap does enter the knowledge-soundness accounting. A malicious prover may choose which of its at
         PoK
most Wmax    attempts to complete, and so which first message the extractor is handed, inflating its conditional
                                                                      (1)
success probability by that factor. That inflation is already inside εABDLOP (§4.3.2) and is the exact analogue
of the role Wmax plays, through the uniform choice of the attempt index τ , in Protocol 17. What Remark B.2
establishes is that the mask loop of Protocol 7 must be capped so that this union bound is over a bounded
number of attempts.
B.4. Proof of Theorem 4.13. The composition Π′DEC ◦ Π′RLC ◦ Π′R1CS is a reduction of knowledge by
Lemma 4.11 and Lemma 4.12; the result follows for ΠLBF . The completeness error is the sum of the three,
by the composition clause of Proposition 2.8, and is computed in Proposition 4.15. Note that Protocols 12
adds nothing to it, their only extra step being the local sampling of Protocol 11, which is rejection-free in the
relevant sense (its restart condition ∥z∥∞ > τλ,ξ s fails only with the negligible probability of Lemma 3.19
and is a local resampling, not an interaction).
   Assume now that K = 1. We prove that ΠLBF satisfies Definition 2.11 in the setting l = 1, R′ = CCS(b, L)
(Definition 2.21), and R = R2 = CEcom (b, L, B̃, Commit)k (Definitions 3.21), and then the same statement
for ΠLBF over the unpadded R′ = CCS◦ (b) of Definition 3.28, which is the reading that gives zero-knowledge.
For this purpose we construct a simulator S which simulates the transcript and output witness for the
interactive reduction Π′DEC ◦ Π′RLC ◦ Π′R1CS given a tuple of instances and public parameters.
Remark B.6 (Naming). Throughout this appendix u1 = (c1 , x1 ) denotes the R′ = CCS instance, which
in the indexing of Definition 2.11 is u2 ; since l = 1 the Rl−1 component of that definition is empty, so
no ambiguity arises. The k pairs the prover samples are indexed i ∈ [K + 1, K + k] and written with the
superscript blind.
The first six hybrids below establish Definition 2.11 over the committed relation and are insensitive to how
the witness was produced. Only the last one, H6 , needs the unpadded reading; we flag this explicitly where
it occurs.
   Before giving the simulator we record precisely which quantities S must be consistent with (recall Defini-
tion 2.21):
      • with l = 1, Definition 2.11 hands S only the public CCS(b, L)K instance u1 = (c1 , x1 ), K = 1, with

       is therefore free for S to choose: the blinding instances (cblind

Remark B.7.
  (1) Some public value must be fixed after Pρ ⃗ is known. More precisely, the verifier computes the output
      instance component as c′ = ρ1 c1 + i∈[k] ρK+i cblind

      also outputs. A simulator that fixed every public value before ρ⃗ and then produced a short z′ satisfying
      this identity would, in particular, have to find a short preimage of ρ1 c1 under L, contradicting the
      relaxed binding of the Ajtai commitment. Late binding of one public value is therefore unavoidable.
      The witness components and the public inputs xblindi    carry a visible norm structure (∥xblind
                                                                                                  i   ∥∞ < b,
      i.e. ternary entries for b = 2), so solving either of them backwards from a linear equation yields a
      value that would not fit the correct distribution and be rejected on sight. However, we still have some
      degree of freedom. By contrast cy ∈ C is a compact Ajtai commitment which is never opened, never
      norm-checked, and enters only V’s linear combination. It is the unique slack variable in the protocol
      that carries no norm obligation.

Summing H0 –H6 gives Equation (4.6) and completes the proof of Theorem 4.13.
  We conclude this section with a series of remarks which should clarify some subtleties.
Remark B.8 (Soundness is not endangered by the late binding). It is worth stating why the ability of S
to fix cy after ρ⃗ does not hand the same power to a cheating prover, since cy is precisely the slack of the
folded C-channel.P In the protocol cy is sent before ρ  ⃗; for a fixed cy , a prover able to exhibit a short z with
L(z) = ρ1 c1 + i ρK+i cblind
                          i    + c y for two distinct challenge  blocks agreeing outside one coordinate yields, by
the extraction of Lemma 4.10, a short opening of the corresponding input commitment, i.e. breaks relaxed
binding of L (MSIS). The simulator escapes this only because Definition 2.11 lets it choose ω ′ , hence ρ        ⃗,
itself: it is never confronted with a random challenge after committing. This is the usual asymmetry behind
HVZK, and it is also the reason both cy and xy must be bound by a pre-challenge message in Protocol 7.
Only their disclosure may be moved after ρ   ⃗ (Remarks B.3 and B.9).
Remark B.9 (Rejected rounds are public). In the interactive setting a rejected attempt of the Rej1 loop of
Protocol 7 is not discarded and it remains part of V ∗ ’s view. This matters, because Lemma 3.20 controls the
distribution of z only conditioned on acceptance. Conditioned on rejection,P        y is distributed as DsnF reweighted
by the complement of the acceptance probability, which depends on v = i ρi zi and hence on the genuine
witness z1 . Likewise, the simulator SABDLOP of Lemma 3.6 produces non-aborting transcripts only.
   This is why the pair (cy , xy ) = (L(y), Lin (y)) is committed rather than sent inside the loop. Since L is a
plain Ajtai commitment, it is not hiding (Remark 2.29), and carries no salt able to absorb the conditioning
above. An in-loop cy would therefore expose a v-dependent quantity on every failed attempt. With the
commitment, each attempt transmits only Cy,0 and {Cy,j }j∈[t] , all of them ABDLOP commitments under
freshly sampled salts and hence hiding under MLWE (§2.5.2). Note that in the argument of Theorem 4.13
they are replaced by commitments to 0, at cost t · εhide + εhide,0 per attempt, the two advantages being
those of two different ABDLOP instances (Remark 4.21). Over the W attempts of the loop this contributes
W · (t εhide + εhide,0 ), with W = O(1) in expectation.
                                      n
   Note that cy ∈ RκF and xy ∈ RF R,in are RF -vectors and are committed in BDLOP message slots. Being
already defined over RF , this requires none of the rank-doubling of §3.3. Its salt block is kept disjoint from
those of {Cy,j } precisely because Cy,0 is the one commitment opened in the clear, and an ABDLOP opening
discloses s2 . Their joint length κ + nR,in exceeds the ℓ message slots of a single commitment, so Cy,0 is
a tuple of Ñ blocks at the ambient parameters, each with a salt pair of its own (Remark 4.21); the hiding
advantage it contributes to Equation (4.6) is correspondingly εhide,0 = Ñ εhide .
   Remark B.8 and the extractor of Lemma 4.10 require that P           cy is fixed before ρ ⃗ is drawn, not that it be
disclosed before ρ⃗. V does not consume cy until it folds c ← i ρi ci + cy , after the loop has terminated.
Binding of Commit pins cy at the moment Cy,0 is sent, so a cheating prover cannot adapt it to ρ                 ⃗. The
norm check on the revealed salts is what supplies this. Without it, the commitment is not binding in any
sense, since for any ∆s2 ̸= 0 one may solve A1 ∆s1 = −A2 ∆s2 and open to cy − B∆s2 , which is exactly
the late binding that Remark B.8 reserves for the simulator. With the check, two accepting openings of
Cy,0 to distinct pairs (cy , xy ) ̸= (c′y , x′y ) differ in some block, and that block yields A1 ∆s1 + A2 ∆s2 = 0
with 0 ̸= ∥(∆s1 , ∆s2 )∥∞ < 2B̃, i.e. a solution to MSISκ,m +m ,2B̃ √(m +m )d at the ambient salt width;
                                                                     1   2        1    2
hence across rewound branches the extractor of Lemma 4.10 recovers the same cy and the same xy , except
with probability ϵbind (B̃), which is what Remark B.3 needs. In fact this one commitment is statistically
binding on the revealed-salt set (for suitable parameters), so the MSIS appeal is conservative. The count
is over differences rather than over salt pairs, and it is read blockwise, at the ambient width: at d = 128,
κ = 11 and m1 + m2 = 68 there are at most 5(m1 +m2 )d candidate differences against q κd/l = 245056 target
values, l = 2 being the number of irreducible factors of X d + 1 modulo q, so a uniform [A1 | A2 ] kills
none of them except with overwhelming probability; the d = 64 set reads the same way, at q κd/l = 238912
against m1 + m2 = 98. Equation (4.19) is the general form, and it holds for every circuit, the width being
independent of nR,in (Remark 4.21). We nonetheless state the argument computationally, since that is the
form the composition consumes. Since the salts are opened in the clear, the verifier reads the bound off
directly, at cost O(Ñ (m1 + m2 )d) and with no appeal to the norm-proving machinery of §3.3.4, which is
needed only for salts that remain hidden. Recall that this is also the case for {Cy,j }j∈[t] , whose bounds
                                               LATTICEBLINDFOLD                                                   77


continue to be enforced in relaxed form by the proof of knowledge at Step 17 and by R2 -membership. As
with the input salts of Protocol 8, this suffices for a single invocation of ΠLBF but not for unbounded iteration
of ΠLBF−Fold , where the mask salts enter the folded salt of every round; cf. Remark 4.8. Only the opening
of Cy,0 , which carries both cy and xy , is processed after ρ    ⃗; both values are already bound before it. The
simulator also samples ω ′ itself (Remark B.7.(2)) and so knows ρ     ⃗(τ ) before producing any prover message.
   With the attempt cap of Protocol 7 in force, the number of rejected attempts appearing in V ∗ ’s view is
bounded by Wmax rather than merely finite, so the accounting below is over at most Wmax terms and the
bound of Theorem 4.13 may be read with the fixed coefficient Wmax ≤ 28 in place of a random attempt count.
Remark B.10 (The simulator is interactive-only). The simulator of Protocol 19 rests on ρ      ⃗(τ ) being a de-
                          ′
terministic function of ω rather than of the transcript, which is exactly how Definition 2.11 models an
honest-but-curious public-coin V ∗ . However, under the Fiat–Shamir instantiation we first discussed in Re-
                                                              (τ )
mark 4.2.(2) this is false. There, ρ ⃗(τ ) = hash(transcript∥Cy,0 ), so computing cy would require ρ⃗(τ̃ ) , which
          (τ̃ )
requires Cy,0 . In order to reconcile the two settings, in the random-oracle model, the simulator instead needs
to programs hash, once per attempt. More precisely, it draws ρ       ⃗(τ ) , computes the attempt as at Step 7, and
                             (τ )
then sets hash(transcript∥Cy,0 ) := ρ⃗(τ ) , aborting on the negligibly likely event that the point has already been
queried. Since at most Wmax attempts occur, at most Wmax programming steps are needed and the abort
probability remains negligible.
   The resulting statement is a ROM one and should not be conflated with the standard-model statement of
Theorem 4.13. Note finally that Fiat–Shamir simplifies the treatment of aborts, since rejected attempts are
never output and the Wmax (t + 1)εhide term of H2 disappears, while complicating the simulator.
